0&0&\cdots&g_{n}\varrho_{n}g_{n}^{-1}\end{array}\right]

such that
g_{i}\varrho_{i}g_{i}^{-1}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{L})
is irreducible.
Write

\tau_{1}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}g_{1}\varrho_{1}g_{1}^{-1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&g_{2}\varrho_{2}g_{2}^{-1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&g_{n}\varrho_{n}g_{n}^{-1}\end{array}\right]

and

\tau_{2}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&h_{12}&\cdots&h_{1n}\\
0&0&\cdots&h_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0\end{array}\right]

Note that
\tau_{2}
is not a group homomorphism but only a map from
\pi_{1}(X)
to
{\rm GL}_{N}(L)
.

For any matrix
B
with values in
L
, we shall write
v(B)
the matrix whose entries are the valuation of the corresponding entries in
B
by
v:L\to\mathbb{R}
. Let us define
M(B)
the lower bound of the entries of
v(B)
. Then for another matrix
A
with values in
L
, one has
M(A+B)\geq\min\{M(A),M(B)\}
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
be a generator of
\pi_{1}(X)
. Let
C
be the lower bound of the entries of
\{v(h_{ij}(x_{k}))\}_{i,j=1,\ldots,n;k=1,\ldots,m}
. We assume that
C<0
, or else it is easy to see that
\tau
is bounded. Note that
\min_{i=1,\ldots,m}M(g_{i}\varrho_{i}g_{i}^{-1}(x_{i}))\geq 0
. It follows that
M(\tau_{1}(x_{i}))\geq 0
for each
x_{i}
.
Then for any
x=x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}
,

\displaystyle M(\tau(x))

\displaystyle=M(\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}=1,2}\tau_{j_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\cdots\tau_{j_{\ell}}(x_{i_{\ell}}))

\displaystyle\geq\min_{{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}=1,2}}\{M(\tau_{j_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\cdots\tau_{j_{\ell}}(x_{i_{\ell}}))\}.

Note that
\tau_{j_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\cdots\tau_{j_{\ell}}(x_{i_{\ell}})=0
if
\#\{k\mid j_{k}=2\}\geq n
since
\tau_{2}(x_{i})
is nilpotent. Hence

M(\tau(x))\geq\min_{{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}=1,2};\#\{k\mid j_{k}=2\}<n}\{M(\tau_{j_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\cdots\tau_{j_{\ell}}(x_{i_{\ell}}))\}.

Since
M(\tau_{1}(x_{i}))\geq 0
for each
x_{i}
, it follows that
M(\tau_{j_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})\cdots\tau_{j_{\ell}}(x_{i_{\ell}})\geq(n-1)C
if
\#\{k\mid j_{k}=2\}<n
. Therefore,
M(\tau(x))\geq(n-1)C
for any
x\in\pi_{1}(X)
.
\tau
is thus bounded. Since
\varrho
is conjugate to
\tau
,
\varrho
is also bounded. We finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

We recall the following facts of character varieties (cf.
[
LM85
, Theorem 1.28]
).

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
K
be an
algebraically closed
field. Then the
K
-points
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the
conjugate classes of semisimple representations
\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K)
. More precisely, if
\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K)\}_{i=1,2}
are two linear representations such that
[\varrho_{1}]=[\varrho_{2}]\in M_{\rm B}(X,N)(K)
, then the semisimplification of
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
are conjugate. ∎

The following result is thus a consequence of
3.6
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
K
be a non-archimedean local field. Let
x\in M_{\rm B}(X,N)(K)
. If
\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{K})\}_{i=1,2}
are two linear representations such that
[\varrho_{1}]=[\varrho_{2}]=x\in M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\bar{K})
, then
\varrho_{1}
is bounded if and only if
\varrho_{2}
is bounded. In other words, for the GIT quotient
\pi:R(X,N)\to M_{\rm B}(X,N)
where
R(X,N)
is the representation variety of
\pi_{1}(X)
into
{\rm GL}_{N}
, for any
x\in M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\bar{K})
, the representations in
\pi^{-1}(x)\subset R(X,N)(\bar{K})
are either all bounded or all unbounded.

Démonstration.

By the assumption and
3.7
, we know that the semisimplificaitons
\varrho_{1}^{ss}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{K})
of
\varrho_{2}^{ss}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{K})
are conjugate by an element
g\in{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{K})
. Therefore, there exists a finite extension
L
of
K
such that
\varrho_{i}^{ss}
and
\varrho_{i}
are all defined in
L
and
g\in{\rm GL}_{N}(L)
. Hence
\varrho_{1}^{ss}
is bounded if and only if
\varrho_{2}^{ss}
is bounded. By
3.6
, we know that
\varrho_{i}^{ss}
is bounded if and only if
\varrho_{i}
is bounded. Therefore, the lemma follows.
∎

We thus can make the following definition.

Definition 3.9

(Class of bounded representations)
.

Let
K
be a non-archimedean local field of characteristic zero. A point
x\in M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\bar{K})
is called
a class of bounded representations
if there exist some
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{K})
(thus any
\varrho
by
3.8
) such that
[\varrho]=x
and
\varrho
is bounded.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety
and let
\mathfrak{C}
be a
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
. Let
f:F\to X
be a morphism from a quasi-projective
normal
variety
F
such that
s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ f(F)
is a point.
Let
\{\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,2}
be reductive representations such that
[\tau_{1}]
and
[\tau_{2}]
are in the same geometric connected component of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then
\tau_{1}\circ\iota
is conjugate to
\tau_{2}\circ\iota
, where
\iota:\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)
is the homomorphism of fundamental groups induced by
f
. In other words,
j(\mathfrak{C})
is zero-dimensional, where
j:M_{\rm B}(X,N)\to M_{\rm B}(F,N)
is the natural morphism of character varieties induced by
\iota:\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)
.

Démonstration.

Let
M_{X}
(resp.
M
) be the moduli space of representations of
\pi_{1}(X)
(resp.
\pi_{1}(F^{\mathrm{norm}})
) in
{\rm GL}_{N}
. Note that
M_{X}
and
M
are both affine schemes of finite type defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
R_{X}
(resp.
R
) be the affine scheme of finite type defined over
\mathbb{Q}
such that
R_{X}(L)={\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(X),{\rm GL}_{N}(L))
(resp.
R(L)={\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(F),{\rm GL}_{N}(L))
) for any field
L/\mathbb{Q}
. Then we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(3.3)

where
\pi:R_{X}\to M_{X}
and
p:R\to M
are the GIT quotient that are both surjective. For any field extension
K/\mathbb{Q}
and any
\varrho\in R_{X}(K)
, we write
[\varrho]:=\pi(\varrho)\in M_{X}(K)
. Let
\mathfrak{R}:=\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{C})
that is a constructible subset defined over some number field
k
. Then
\tau_{i}\in\mathfrak{R}(\mathbb{C})
.

Claim 3.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
be any geometric irreducible component of
\mathfrak{R}
. Then
j\circ\pi(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
is zero dimensional.

Démonstration.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
j\circ\pi(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
is positive-dimensional. If we replace
k
by a finite extension, we may assume that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is defined over
k
.
Since
M
is an affine
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme of finite type, it follows that there exist a
k
-morphism
\psi:M\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
such that the image
\psi\circ j\circ\pi(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
is Zariski dense in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. After replacing
k
by a finite extension, we can find a locally closed irreducible curve
C\subset\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
such that the restriction
\psi\circ j\circ\pi|_{C}:C\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
is a generically finite
k
-morphism. We take a Zariski open subset
U\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}
such that
\psi\circ j\circ\pi|_{C}
is finite over
U
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime ideal of the ring of integer
\mathcal{O}_{k}
and let
K
be its non-archimedean completion. In the following, we shall work over
K
.

Let
x\in U(K)
be a point, and let
y\in C(\bar{K})
be a point over
x
. Then
y
is defined over some extension of
K
whose extension degree is bounded by the degree of
\psi\circ j\circ\pi|_{C}:C\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Note that there are only finitely many such field extensions. Hence there exists a finite extension
L/K
such that the points over
U(K)
are all contained in
C(L)
. Since
U(K)\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}(L)
is unbounded, the image
\psi\circ j\circ\pi(C(L))\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}(L)
is unbounded.

Let
R_{0}
be the set of bounded representations in
R(L)
. Recall that by
[
Yam10
]
,
M_{0}:=p(R_{0})
is compact in
M(L)
with respect to analytic topology, hence
M_{0}
is bounded by
3.4
. By
3.4
once again,
\psi(M_{0})
is a bounded subset in
\mathbb{A}^{1}(L)
. Recall that
\psi\circ j\circ\pi(C(L))\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}(L)
is unbounded. Therefore, there exists
\varrho\in C(L)
such that
\psi\circ j([\varrho])\not\in\psi(M_{0})
. Note that
[\varrho\circ\iota]=j([\varrho])
by (
). Hence
[\varrho\circ\iota]\not\in M_{0}
which implies that
\varrho\circ\iota\not\in R_{0}
. By the definition of
R_{0}
,
\varrho\circ\iota
is unbounded.

Let
\varrho^{ss}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{L})
be the semisimplification of
\varrho
.
Then
[\varrho]=[\varrho^{ss}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\bar{L})
by
3.7
. Therefore,
[\varrho\circ\iota]=[\varrho^{ss}\circ\iota]\in M(\bar{L})
by (
). By
3.8
,
\varrho^{ss}\circ\iota:\pi_{1}(F)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{L})
is also unbounded. Note that
\varrho^{ss}\circ\iota
is reductive by
1.7
. Since
\pi_{1}(F)
is finitely generated, there exist a finite extension
L^{\prime}
of
L
such that
\varrho^{ss}
is defined over
L^{\prime}
.
However, by
3.2
,
\varrho^{ss}\circ\iota
is always bounded. We obtain a contradiction and thus
j\circ\pi(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
is zero dimensional.

We can also apply
[
Kem78
, Corollary 4.3]
instead of
3.8
. As
\varrho\in C(L)
, its image
[\varrho]\in M_{X}(L)
. Consider the fiber
\pi^{-1}([\varrho])
which is an
L
-variety. Its closed orbit
is defined over
L
by Galois descent. As
\pi^{-1}([\varrho])
contains the
L
-point
\varrho
, the closed orbit in
\pi^{-1}([\varrho])

has an
L
-point
\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(L)
as well by
[
Kem78
, Corollary 4.3]
. By
3.7
,
\varrho^{\prime}
is reductive and
[\varrho^{\prime}]=[\varrho]
. Hence
[\varrho^{\prime}\circ\iota]=[\varrho\circ\iota]\not\in M_{0}
. Therefore,
\varrho^{\prime}\circ\iota:\pi_{1}(F)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(L)
is unbounded by our definition of
M_{0}
. However, by the definition of
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
in
3.1
,
\varrho^{\prime}\circ\iota
is always bounded. We obtain a contradiction and thus
j\circ\pi(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
is zero dimensional.
∎

Let
\{\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,2}
be reductive representations such that
[\tau_{1}]
and
[\tau_{2}]
are contained in the same geometric connected component
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. We aim to prove that
j(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
is a point in
M(\mathbb{C})
.

Consider a geometric irreducible component
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
. We can choose a geometric irreducible component
Z
of
\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime})
such that
\pi(Z)
is dense in
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime}
. It follows that
Z
is an irreducible component of
\mathfrak{R}(\mathbb{C})
. By
3.11
, we know that
j\circ\pi(Z)
is a point in
M(\mathbb{C})
. Thus,
j(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime\prime})
is also a point in
M(\mathbb{C})
.

Consequently,
j(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
is a point in
M(\mathbb{C})
. As a result, we have
[\tau_{1}\circ\iota]=j([\tau_{1}])=j([\tau_{2}])=[\tau_{2}\circ\iota]
. By
1.7
,
\tau_{1}\circ\iota
and
\tau_{2}\circ\iota
are reductive, and according to
3.7
, they are conjugate to each other.
We have established the proposition.

∎

We will need the following lemma on the intersection of kernels of representations.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
X
be a quasi-projective normal variety and let
\mathfrak{C}
be a constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\cap_{[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}}\ker\varrho=\cap_{[\varrho]\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}}\ker\varrho,

(3.4)

where
\varrho
’s are reductive representations of
\pi_{1}(X)
into
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

Démonstration.

Let
M_{X}
be the moduli space of representation of
\pi_{1}(X)
in
{\rm GL}_{N}
. Let
R_{X}
be the affine scheme of finite type such that
R(L)={\rm Hom}(\pi_{1}(X),N)(L)
for any field
\mathbb{Q}\subset L
. We write
M:=M_{X}(\mathbb{C})
and
R:=R_{X}(\mathbb{C})
. Then the GIT quotient
\pi:R\to M
is a surjective morphism. It follows that
\pi^{-1}(\mathfrak{C})
is a
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
-invariant subset where
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
R
by the conjugation. Define
H:=\cap_{[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}}\ker\varrho
, where
\varrho
’s are reductive representations of
\pi_{1}(X)
into
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
. Pick any
\gamma\in H
. Then the set
Z_{\gamma}:=\{\varrho\in R\mid\varrho(\gamma)=1\}
is a Zariski closed subset of
R
. Moreover,
Z_{\gamma}
is
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
-invariant. Define
Z:=\cap_{\gamma\in H}Z_{\gamma}
. Then
Z
is also
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
-invariant. Therefore,
\pi(Z)
is also a Zariski closed subset of
M
by
[
Muk03
, Proposition 5.10]
. Note that
\mathfrak{C}\subset\pi(Z)
. Therefore,
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}\subset\pi(Z)
. Note that for any reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\pi(Z)
, we have
\varrho(\gamma)=1
for any
\gamma\in H
. It follows that (
3.4
) holds.
∎

Lastly, let us prove the main result of this subsection. This result will serve as a crucial cornerstone in the proofs of
C
,
A
and
D
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety. Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
, defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, such that
\mathfrak{C}
is invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action. When
X
is non-compact, we further assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is closed. Then there exist reductive representations
\{\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
such that each
\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS, and for a morphism
\iota:Z\to X
from any quasi-projective
normal
variety
Z
with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\iota(Z)
being a point, the following properties hold :

(i)

For
\sigma:=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
,
\iota^{*}\sigma(\pi_{1}(Z))
is discrete in
\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

For each reductive representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
,
\iota^{*}\tau
is conjugate to some
\iota^{*}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
.

(iii)

For each
\sigma_{i}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}
, there exists a reductive representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
such that
\iota^{*}\tau
is conjugate to
\iota^{*}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
.

(iv)

For every
i=1,\ldots,m
, we have

\displaystyle\cap_{[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}}\ker\varrho\subset\ker\sigma_{i}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}}

(3.5)

where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
varies among all reductive representations such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.

Démonstration.

Let
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{C}_{\ell}
be all geometric connected components of
\mathfrak{C}
which are defined over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
. We can pick reductive representations
\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}
such that
[\varrho_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
for every
i
. Since
\pi_{1}(X)
is finitely generated, there exists a number field
k
which is a Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(k)
for every
\varrho_{i}
.

Let
{\rm Ar}(k)
be all archimedean places of
k
with
w_{1}
the identity map. Then for any
w\in{\rm Ar}(k)
there exists
a\in{\rm Gal}(k/\mathbb{Q})
such that
w=w_{1}\circ a
. Note that
\mathfrak{C}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Then
\mathfrak{C}
is invariant under the conjugation
a
. Therefore, for any
w:k\to\mathbb{C}
in
{\rm Ar}(k)
, letting
\varrho_{i,w}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be the composition
w\circ\varrho_{i}
, we have
[\varrho_{i,w}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, we consider the
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action
\varrho_{i,w,t}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
of
\varrho_{i,w}
defined in
section

2.4
. Then
\varrho_{i,w,t}
is also reductive by the arguments in
section

2.4
. Since we assume that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
is invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action, it follows that
[\varrho_{i,w,t}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. By
2.7
,
[\varrho_{i,w,t}]
is a continuous deformation of
[\varrho_{i,w}]
.
Hence they are in the same geometric connected component of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
, and by
proposition

3.10
we conclude that
[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w,t}]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}]
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
.

We first assume that
X
is compact. According to
[
Sim92
]
,
\lim\limits_{t\to 0}[\varrho_{i,w,t}]
exists, and there exists a reductive
\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}]=\lim\limits_{t\to 0}[\varrho_{i,w,t}]
. Moreover,
\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS. Therefore,
[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}]=\lim\limits_{t\to 0}[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w,t}]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}]
. Since
[\varrho_{i,w,t}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, it follows that
[\varrho_{i,w}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}}]\in\overline{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathbb{C})
. By
eq.

3.4
, we conclude

\displaystyle\cap_{[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}}\ker\varrho\subset\ker\varrho_{i,w}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}}.

(3.6)

Assume now
X
is non-compact. As we assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is closed and invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action, by
2.8
, we can choose a reductive representation
\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that

—

it underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS ;

—

[\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}]
and
[\varrho_{i,w}]
are in the same geometric connected component of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.

Note that (
3.6
) is satisfied automatically. By
proposition

3.10
, we have
[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}]
.

In summary, we construct reductive representations
\{\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,\ldots,k;w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}
in both compact and non-compact cases. Each of these representations underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS and satisfies
[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}]
and (
3.6
).

Let
v
be any non-archimedean place of
k
and
k_{v}
be the non-archimedean completion of
k
with respect to
v
. Write
\varrho_{i,v}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(k_{v})
the induced representation by
\varrho_{i}
. By the construction of
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
, it follows that
\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,v}(\pi_{1}(Z))
is bounded. Therefore, we have a factorization

\iota^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(Z)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{k}).

Note that
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{k})\to\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is a discrete subgroup by
[
Zim84
, Proposition 6.1.3]
. It follows that for the product representation

\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}:\pi_{1}(Z)\to\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C}),

its image is discrete.

Since
Z
is normal, by
1.7
, both
\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}
and
\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}
are reductive. Recall that
[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}]
. It follows that
\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}
is conjugate to
\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}
by
3.7
. Consequently,
\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}:\pi_{1}(Z)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has discrete image. Consider the product representation of
\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}

\sigma:=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C}).

Then
\sigma
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS and
\iota^{*}\sigma:\pi_{1}(Z)\to\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\prod_{w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has discrete image.

Let
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be any reductive representation such that
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. Then
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(\mathbb{C})
for some
i
. By
proposition

3.10
, it follows that

[\iota^{*}\tau]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w_{1}}]=[\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w_{1}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}].

By
1.7
and
3.7
once again,
\iota^{*}\tau
is conjugate to
\iota^{*}\varrho_{i,w_{1}}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}
. The proposition is proved if we let
\{\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
be
\{\varrho_{i,w}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell;w\in{\rm Ar}(k)}
.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

In the proof of
proposition

3.13
, we take the Galois conjugate of
\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)
under
a\in{\rm Gal}(k/\mathbb{Q})
. If
\mathfrak{C}
is not defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, it is not known that
a(\mathfrak{C})\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)
is
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-invariant. This is why we include the assumption that
\mathfrak{C}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
in our proof, whereas Eyssidieux disregarded such a condition in
[
Eys04
]
. It seems that this condition should also be necessary in
[
Eys04
]
.

3.2.
Infinite monodromy at infinity

When considering a non-compact quasi-projective variety
X
, it is important to note that the Shafarevich conjecture fails in simple examples. For instance, take
X:=A\backslash\{0\}
, where
A
is an abelian surface. Its universal covering
\widetilde{X}
is
\mathbb{C}^{2}-\Gamma
, where
\Gamma
is a lattice in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Then
\widetilde{X}
is not holomorphically convex. Therefore, additional conditions on the fundamental groups at infinity are necessary to address this issue.

Definition 3.15

(Infinity monodromy at infinity)
.

Let
X
be a quasi-projective normal variety and let
\overline{X}
be a projective compactification of
X
. We say a subset
M\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})

has infinite monodromy at infinity
if for any holomorphic map
\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{X}
with
\gamma^{-1}(\overline{X}\setminus X)=\{0\}
, there exists a reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in M
and
\gamma^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has infinite image.

Definition 3.16

(quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity)
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety. Let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a representation.
Say
\varrho
has
quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity
with respect to the partial compactification
X^{\prime}
of
X
if for every boundary divisor
D\subset X^{\prime}\backslash X
the local monodromy is finite and for every
f:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to X
such that
f(0)\not\in X^{\prime}
,
f^{*}\varrho:\mathbb{Z}\to\pi_{1}(X)
is infinite.

Note that
3.15
does not depend on the projective compactification of
X
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a
proper
morphism between quasi-projective normal varieties. If
M\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
has infinite monodromy at infinity, then
f^{*}M\subset M_{\rm B}(Y,N)(\mathbb{C})
also has infinite monodromy at infinity.

Démonstration.

We take projective compactification
\overline{X}
and
\overline{Y}
of
X
and
Y
respectively such that
f
extends to a morphism
\bar{f}:\overline{Y}\to\overline{X}
. Let
\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{Y}
be any holomorphic map with
\gamma^{-1}(\overline{Y}\setminus Y)=\{0\}
. Then
\bar{f}\circ\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{X}
satisfies
(\bar{f}\circ\gamma)^{-1}(\overline{X}\setminus X)=\{0\}
as
f
is proper. Then by
3.15
there exists a reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in M
and
\gamma^{*}(f^{*}\varrho)=(f\circ\gamma)^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has infinite image. The lemma follows.
∎

We have a precise local characterization of a representation with infinite monodromy at infinity.

Lemma 3.18
.

Consider a smooth quasi-projective variety
X
along with a smooth projective compactification
\overline{X}
, where
D:=\overline{X}\backslash X
is a simple normal crossing divisor. A set
M\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
has infinite monodromy at infinity is equivalent to the following : for any
x\in D
, there exists an admissible coordinate
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
x
with
U\cap D=(z_{1}\cdots z_{k}=0)
such that for any
k
-tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{k}
, there exists a reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in M(\mathbb{C})
and
\varrho(\gamma_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{k}^{i_{k}})\neq 0
, where
\gamma_{i}
is the anti-clockwise
loop around the origin in the i-th factor of
U\setminus D\simeq(\mathbb{D}^{*})^{k}\times\mathbb{D}^{n-k}
. For such condition we will say that
\varrho

has infinite monodromy at
x
.

Démonstration.

For any holomorphic map
f:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{X}
with
f^{-1}(D)=\{0\}
, let
x:=f(0)
which lies on
D
. We take an admissible coordinate
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
x
in the lemma. Then
f({\mathbb{D}_{2\varepsilon}})\subset U
for some small
\varepsilon>0
. We can write
f(t)=(f_{1}(t),\ldots,f_{n}(t))
such that
f_{1}(0)=\cdots=f_{k}(0)=0
and
f_{i}(0)\neq 0
for
i=k+1,\ldots,n
. Denote by
m_{i}:={\rm ord}_{0}f_{i}
the vanishing order of
f_{i}(t)
at
0
. Consider the anti-clockwise loop
\gamma
defined by
\theta\mapsto\varepsilon e^{i\theta}
which generates
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{2\varepsilon}^{*})
. Then
f\circ\gamma
is homotopy equivalent to
\gamma_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{k}^{m_{k}}
in
\pi_{1}(U\backslash D)
. If
M
has infinite monodromy at infinity, by
3.15
there exists a reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in M(\mathbb{C})
and
f^{*}\varrho(\gamma)\neq 0
. This is equivalent to that
\varrho(\gamma_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{k}^{m_{k}})\neq 0
. The lemma is proved.
∎

3.15
presents a stringent condition that is not be practically applicable in many situations. To address this issue, we establish the following result :

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety. Assume that
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is a linear representation. Then there exists a smooth partial compactification
X^{\prime}
of
X
such that

(i)

\varrho
has
quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity
with respect to the partial compactification
X^{\prime}
of
X
;

(ii)

X^{\prime}\backslash X
is simple normal crossing divisor.

In particular, if
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is torsion free, then
\varrho
extends to a representation
\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(X^{\prime})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with infinite monodromy at infinity.

Démonstration.

We first introduce the definition of
representation systems
. Let
\overline{X}
be a smooth projective compactification such that the boundary
\overline{X}\setminus X
is a simple normal crossing divisor
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}D_{i}
. If
D_{i_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap D_{i_{n}}\neq\varnothing
for some
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}\subset\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, then for a generic point
x\in D_{i_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap D_{i_{n}}
, locally we have an coordinate system
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})
centered at
x
such that
\overline{X}\cap U
is a polydisk with
D_{i_{k}}\cap U=(z_{k}=0)
.
We then have
U\cap X=(\mathbb{D}^{*})^{n}\times\mathbb{D}^{d-n}
such that
\pi_{1}(U\cap X)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and the local monodromy of
\varrho
around
x
is a representation
\varrho:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that
\varrho(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
may have torsion
T\subset\varrho(\mathbb{Z}^{n})
.
Taking the quotient, we have
\mathbb{Z}^{m}\simeq\varrho(\mathbb{Z}^{n})/T
for some
m
.
Then the local monodromy
\varrho
induces a linear map
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, which is represented by a matrix in
M_{m,n}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Let
A=(\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n})\subset M_{m,n}(\mathbb{Z})
be a matrix, where each
\bm{a}_{i}
is a column vector.
Given
J\subset\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, we denote by
A[J]
a submatrix which consists of column vectors
\bm{a}_{j}
for
j\in J
.
Let
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
be a set of irreducible components of the boundary divisors
\overline{X}-X
.
We say that
(A,\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\})
is a
representation system
on
\overline{X}
if for any
J\subset\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
such that
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}\not=\emptyset
, the local monodromy at
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}

modulo the torsion
is represented by the matrix
A[J]
.

Let
D,D^{\prime}
be distinct irreducible components of
\partial X
such that
D\cap D^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
.
We consider the blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{D\cap D^{\prime}}\overline{X}\to\overline{X}
.
(We shall call these blow-ups
admissible blow-ups
.)
Given a representation system
S=(A,\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\})
, where
A=(\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n})\in M_{m,n}(\mathbb{Z})
, we set a system
S^{\prime}
on
\mathrm{Bl}_{D\cap D^{\prime}}\overline{X}
as follows.

—

When
D,D^{\prime}\in\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
, we take
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, where
i\not=j
, such that
D=D_{i}
and
D^{\prime}=D_{j}
.
We set
A^{\prime}=(\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n},\bm{a}_{i}+\bm{a}_{j})\in M_{m,n+1}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Let
D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime}
be the strict transforms of
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
, respectively, and let
D_{n+1}^{\prime}
be the exceptional divisor.
We set
S^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},\{D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime},D_{n+1}^{\prime}\})
.

—

Otherwise, we set
S^{\prime}=(A,\{D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime}\})
, where
D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime}
are the strict transforms of
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
, respectively.

Claim 3.20
.

When
S
is a representation system on
\overline{X}
, then
S^{\prime}
is a representation system on
\mathrm{Bl}_{D\cap D^{\prime}}\overline{X}
.

Proof of
3.20
.

There are two cases as above.
The first case is that
D,D^{\prime}\in\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
.
Assume
D=D_{j_{1}}
and
D^{\prime}=D_{j_{2}}
, where
j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Let
J\subset\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
so that
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
.
If
J\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
, then
(\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}^{\prime})\backslash D_{n+1}^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
.
Hence the local monodromy at
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}^{\prime}
coincides with
A[J]=A^{\prime}[J]
.
If
n+1\in J
, we set
J^{\prime}=J\backslash\{j_{1},j_{2},n+1\}
.
Then we have
\bigcap_{j\in J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{1},j_{2}\}}D_{j}\not=\emptyset
.
Then the local monodromy at
\bigcap_{j\in J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{1},n+1\}}D_{j}^{\prime}
is
A^{\prime}[J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{1},n+1\}]
.
Similarly the local monodromy at
\bigcap_{j\in J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{2},n+1\}}D_{j}^{\prime}
is
A^{\prime}[J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{2},n+1\}]
.
By
D_{j_{1}}^{\prime}\cap D_{j_{2}}^{\prime}=\emptyset
, we have
\{j_{1},j_{2}\}\not\subset J
.
Hence either
J\subset J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{1},n+1\}
or
J\subset J^{\prime}\cup\{j_{2},n+1\}
.
Hence the local monodromy at
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}^{\prime}
coincides with
A^{\prime}[J]
.

Next we consider the second case that
\{D,D^{\prime}\}\not\subset\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
.
Then
(\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}^{\prime})\backslash E\not=\emptyset
, where
E
is the exceptional divisor.
Hence the local monodromy at
\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}^{\prime}
coincides with
A[J]=A^{\prime}[J]
.
∎

Given a representation system
S=(A,\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\})
, we introduce notations
\mu(S)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\alpha(S)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\beta(S)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\gamma(S)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
as follows.

—

\mu(S)
is the maximum
\mu
such that there exists
(i,j)
with
D_{i}\cap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
and
a_{\mu,i}a_{\mu,j}<0
.
We set
\mu(S)=0
if there is no such
i,j
for all
\mu=1,\ldots,m
.

—

\alpha(S)
is the maximum of
|a_{\mu(S),i}-a_{\mu(S),j}|
among all
(i,j)
with
D_{i}\cap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
and
a_{\mu(S),i}a_{\mu(S),j}<0
.
We set
\alpha(S)=0
if
\mu(S)=0
.

—

\beta(S)
is the number of
(i,j)
such that
D_{i}\cap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
,
a_{\mu(S),i}a_{\mu(S),j}<0
and
|a_{\mu(S),i}-a_{\mu(S),j}|=\alpha(S)
.
We set
\beta(S)=0
if
\mu(S)=0
.

—

We set
\gamma(S)=(\mu(S),\alpha(S),\beta(S))\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
.

Claim 3.21
.

There exists a sequence
\overline{X}^{(n)}\to\overline{X}^{(n-1)}\to\cdots\to\overline{X}
of admissible blow-ups such that
\mu(S^{(n)})=0
.

Proof of
3.21
.

If
\mu(S)=0
, there is nothing to prove.
So suppose
\mu(S)>0
.
Then we take
i,j
such that
D_{i}\cap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
,
a_{\mu(S),i}a_{\mu(S),j}<0
and
|a_{\mu(S),i}-a_{\mu(S),j}|=\alpha(S)
.
We take an admissible blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{D_{i}\cap D_{j}}\overline{X}\to\overline{X}
.
Then
S^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},\{D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime},D_{n+1}^{\prime}\})
.

We claim
\gamma(S^{\prime})<\gamma(S)
under the lexicographic order in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
.
Let
\mu>\mu(S)
.
Then for all
D_{s}\cap D_{t}\not=\emptyset
, we have
a_{\mu s}a_{\mu t}\geq 0
.
In particular, no sign change occurs in the three numbers
a_{\mu i}+a_{\mu j}
,
a_{\mu i}
and
a_{\mu j}
.
This shows
\mu(S^{\prime})<\mu
.
Hence
\mu(S^{\prime})\leq\mu(S)
.
We have
a_{\mu(S),i}a_{\mu(S),j}<0
.
Hence either
a_{\mu(S),i}<a_{\mu(S),i}+a_{\mu(S),j}<a_{\mu(S),j}
or
a_{\mu(S),i}>a_{\mu(S),i}+a_{\mu(S),j}>a_{\mu(S),j}
.
In each case, we have
\alpha(S^{\prime})\leq\alpha(S)
.
Moreover, when
\alpha(S^{\prime})=\alpha(S)
, the condition
D_{i}^{\prime}\cap D_{j}^{\prime}=\emptyset
yields
\beta(S^{\prime})<\beta(S)
.
Thus we have proved
\gamma(S^{\prime})<\gamma(S)
.

Now we have a sequence of admissible blow-ups
\overline{X}\leftarrow\overline{X}^{(1)}\leftarrow\cdots
so that
\gamma(S)>\gamma(S^{(1)})>\cdots
.
This sequence should terminate to get the desired
\overline{X}^{(n)}
.
∎

Claim 3.22
.

Assume
\mu(S)=0
.
Then after an admissible blow-up
\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
, we still have
\mu(S^{\prime})=0
.

Proof of
3.22
.

Let
\overline{X}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Bl}_{D\cap D^{\prime}}\overline{X}
and
S=(A,\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\})
.
There are two cases.
The first case is that
\{D,D^{\prime}\}\subset\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
.
Then
S^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},\{D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime},D_{n+1}^{\prime}\})
.
Suppose
D_{i}^{\prime}\cap D_{j}^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
.
If
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, then
D_{i}\cap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
.
Thus we have
a_{\mu,i}a_{\mu,j}\geq 0
for all
\mu
.
If
j=n+1
, then
D_{i}\cap D_{j_{1}}\not=\emptyset
and
D_{i}\cap D_{j_{2}}\not=\emptyset
, where
D=D_{j_{1}}
and
D^{\prime}=D_{j_{2}}
.
Hence
a_{\mu,i}a_{\mu,j_{1}}\geq 0
and
a_{\mu,i}a_{\mu,j_{2}}\geq 0
for all
\mu
.
Hence
a_{\mu,i}a_{\mu,n+1}=a_{\mu,i}(a_{\mu,j_{1}}+a_{\mu,j_{2}})\geq 0
for all
\mu
.
Hence
\mu(S^{\prime})=0
in the case
D,D^{\prime}\in\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
.

Next we assume
\{D,D^{\prime}\}\not\subset\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
.
Then
S^{\prime}=(A,\{D_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,D_{n}^{\prime}\})
.
If
D_{i}^{\prime}\cap D_{j}^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
, then
D_{i}\cap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
.
Thus we have
a_{\mu,i}a_{\mu,j}\geq 0
for all
\mu
.
Hence
\mu(S^{\prime})=0

∎

We consider a finite set
\Lambda=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{\nu}\}
of representation systems.
We say that
\Lambda
is
maximum
if for every
D^{1},\ldots,D^{l}
with
\bigcap_{j=1}^{l}D^{j}\not=\emptyset
, there exists
S_{\lambda}=(A_{\lambda},\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n_{\lambda}}\})\in\Lambda
such that
D^{1},\ldots,D^{l}\in\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n_{\lambda}}\}
.
For an admissible blow-up
\overline{X}^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
, we get the finite set
\Lambda^{\prime}=\{S_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,S_{\nu}^{\prime}\}
of representation systems on
\overline{X}^{\prime}
.

Claim 3.23
.

If
\Lambda
is maximum, then
\Lambda^{\prime}
is maximum.

Proof of
3.23
.

Let
\mathrm{Bl}_{D\cap D^{\prime}}\overline{X}\to\overline{X}
be the admissible blow-up.
Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{l}
satisfy
\bigcap_{j=1}^{l}F_{j}\not=\emptyset
, where
F_{j}
is a boundary divisor on
\mathrm{Bl}_{D\cap D^{\prime}}\overline{X}
.

There are the two cases.
The first case is that
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{l}\}
contains the exceptional divisor, say
F_{l}
.
Then
F_{1},\ldots,F_{l-1}
are the strict transforms of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{l-1}
.
Then we have
(\bigcap_{j=1}^{l-1}G_{j})\cap D\cap D^{\prime}\not=\emptyset
.
Hence there exists
S_{\lambda}\in\Lambda
such that
\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{l-1},D,D^{\prime}\}\subset\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n_{\lambda}}\}
.
Thus the set of divisors in
S_{\lambda}^{\prime}
contains the strict transforms
F_{1},\ldots,F_{l-1}
and the exceptional divisor
F_{l}
.

The second case is that
\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{l}\}
does not contain the exceptional divisor.
Then
F_{1},\ldots,F_{l}
are the strict transforms of
G_{1},\ldots,G_{l}
.
We have
\bigcap_{j=1}^{l}G_{j}\not=\emptyset
.
Hence there exists
S_{\lambda}\in\Lambda
such that
\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{l}\}\subset\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n_{\lambda}}\}
.
Thus the set of divisors on
S_{\lambda}^{\prime}
contains the strict transforms
F_{1},\ldots,F_{l}
.
Hence we have proved that
\Lambda^{\prime}
is maximum.
∎

Claim 3.24
.

Assume that there exists a finite set
\Lambda=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{\nu}\}
of representation systems which is maximum and satisfies
\mu(S_{\lambda})=0
for all
S_{\lambda}
.
Assume that
f:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to X
has finite monodromy.
Then for every irreducible boundary divisor
D
with
f(0)\in D
, the local monodromy with respect to
D
is finite.

Proof of
3.24
.

Let
D^{1},\ldots,D^{l}
be the boundary divisors which contains
f(0)
.
Then
\bigcap_{j=1}^{l}D^{j}\not=\emptyset
.
Hence there exists
S_{\lambda}=(A_{\lambda},\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n_{\lambda}}\})\in\Lambda
such that
\{D^{1},\ldots,D^{l}\}\subset\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n_{\lambda}}\})
.
We take
J\subset\{1,\ldots,n_{\lambda}\}
such that
\{D^{1},\ldots,D^{l}\}=\{D_{j}\}_{j\in J}
.
We set
b_{j}=\mathrm{ord}_{0}f^{*}D_{j}>0
for
j\in J
.
Then since
A_{\lambda}[J]
is a local monodromy representation modulo the torsion around
f(0)
, we have
\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\bm{a}_{j}=0
, where
A_{\lambda}=(\bm{a}_{1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n_{\lambda}})
.

We claim that
A_{\lambda}[J]
is a zero matrix.
Indeed otherwise, we may take
a_{\mu j_{1}}\not=0
from
A_{\lambda}[J]
.
Then by
\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}\bm{a}_{j}=0
, we may take
a_{\mu j_{2}}\not=0
so that
a_{\mu j_{1}}a_{\mu j_{2}}<0
, for
b_{j}>0
for all
j\in J
.
By
D_{j_{1}}\cap D_{j_{2}}\not=\emptyset
, this contradicts to
\mu(S_{\lambda})=0
.
Hence
A_{\lambda}[J]
is a zero matrix.
Thus the local monodromy around
D_{j}
is finite for all
j\in J
.
∎

Let
\overline{X}
be a compactification such that the boundary divisor
\overline{X}-X
is simple normal crossing.
We shall show that after a sequence
\overline{X}^{(n)}\to\overline{X}^{(n-1)}\to\cdots\to\overline{X}
of admissible blow-ups,
\overline{X}^{(n)}
becomes a compactification with the good property.

We consider all sets
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
of boundary divisors such that
\bigcap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
and the monodromy matrices
A
for these
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
and construct
\Lambda=\{S_{\lambda}\}
.
This
\Lambda
is maximum.

By
3.21
and
3.22
, there exists a sequence
\overline{X}^{(n)}\to\overline{X}^{(n-1)}\to\cdots\to\overline{X}
of admissible blow-ups such that
\mu(S_{\lambda}^{(n)})=0
for all
S_{\lambda}
.
Note that
\Lambda^{(n)}=\{S_{\lambda}^{(n)}\}
is maximum (cf.
3.23
).
We replace
\overline{X}
by
\overline{X}^{(n)}
and
\Lambda
by
\Lambda^{(n)}
.
Then
\overline{X}
and
\Lambda
satisfy the assumption of
3.24
.
Hence if
D
is a boundary divisor with infinite local monodromy and
f:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to X
satisfies
f(0)\in D
, then
f
has infinite monodromy.
Now we remove all
D
from
\overline{X}
such that local monodromy at
D
is infinite to get a desired partial compactification
X^{\prime}
of
X
.

If
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is torsion free, then the local monodromy is trivial around any irreducible divisor of
X^{\prime}\backslash X
. Hence
\varrho
can be extended to a representation over
X^{\prime}
with infinite monodromy at infinity.
∎

3.3.
Some lemmas on finitely generated linear groups

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
M
be an closed subvariety of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
. Let
H:=\bigcap_{\tau[\tau]\in M(\mathbb{C})}\ker\tau
, where
\tau
ranges over all reductive representations in
M(\mathbb{C})
. Then there exists a reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in M(\mathbb{C})
and
\ker\varrho=H
.

Démonstration.

Pick any
\gamma\in H
. Then the set
Z_{\gamma}:=\{\varrho\in R_{\rm B}(X,N)\mid\varrho(\gamma)=1\}
is a Zariski closed subset of the representation variety
R_{\rm B}(X,N)
. Moreover,
Z_{\gamma}
is
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
-invariant. Therefore,
W_{\gamma}:=\pi(Z_{\gamma})
is also a Zariski closed subset of
M
by
[
Muk03
, Proposition 5.10]
.

Claim 3.26
.

For any reductive
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
and any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
,
\tau(\gamma)=1
if and only if
[\tau]\in W_{\gamma}
.

Démonstration.

If
\tau(\gamma)=1
, then by the definition of
Z_{\gamma}
we know that
\tau\in Z_{\gamma}
. Hence
[\tau]\in W_{\gamma}
.

If
[\tau]\in W_{\gamma}
, then there exists (possibly non-reductive)
\tau^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
\tau^{\prime}\in Z_{\gamma}
and
[\tau^{\prime}]=[\tau]
. Hence
\tau^{\prime}(\gamma)=1
and the semisimplification of
\tau^{\prime}
is conjugate to
\tau
.
Hence
\tau(\gamma)=1
. The claim is proved.
∎

Claim 3.27
.

For any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
,
M\subset W_{\gamma}
if and only if
\gamma\in H
.

Démonstration.

If
\gamma\in H
, it is easy to see that
M\subset W_{\gamma}
.

Assume now
\gamma\notin H
. Then there exists a reductive
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\tau]\in M(\mathbb{C})
and
\tau(w)\neq 1
.
Note that for any
\tau^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, if
[\tau^{\prime}]=[\tau]
, then the semisimplification of
\tau^{\prime}
is conjugate to
\tau
. It follows that
\tau^{\prime}(w)\neq 1
. Therefore,
\tau^{\prime}\not\in Z_{\gamma}
. It follows that
[\tau]\not\in W_{\gamma}
.
∎

Therefore,
M\setminus\bigcup_{\gamma\notin H}W_{\gamma}
is non-empty since
M
is irreducible. We pick a reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in M\setminus\bigcup_{\gamma\notin H}W_{\gamma}
. Then for any
\gamma\not\in H
,
\varrho(\gamma)\neq 1
as
[\varrho]\not\in W_{\gamma}
thanks to
3.26
. Hence
\ker\varrho\subset H
.
Note that
H\subset\ker\varrho
. The lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 3.28
.

Consider any subset
\Sigma\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
. Let
M
be the Zariski closure of
\Sigma
. Let
H_{1}:=\bigcap_{[\tau]\in M(\mathbb{C})}\ker\varrho
, where
\tau
ranges over all reductive representations in
M(\mathbb{C})
, and
H_{2}:=\bigcap_{[\tau]\in\Sigma}\ker\varrho
, where
\tau
ranges over all reductive representations in
\Sigma
. Then
H_{1}=H_{2}
. In particular,
\pi_{1}(X)/H_{2}
is a finitely presented linear group.

Démonstration.

For any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
, we let
W_{\gamma}
be the Zariski closed subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
defined in the proof of
3.25
. Consider
M_{0}:=\bigcap_{\gamma\in H_{2}}W_{\gamma}
. Then
M_{0}
is a Zariski closed subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
. For any reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\varrho]\in\Sigma
, by
3.26
we have
[\varrho]\in M_{0}(\mathbb{C})
. Hence
\Sigma\subset M_{0}(\mathbb{C})
.

For any reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
\varrho\in M_{0}(\mathbb{C})
, by
3.26
again we have
\varrho(\gamma)=1
for any
\gamma\in H_{2}
. Hence
H_{2}\subset\ker\varrho
. Recall that
\Sigma\subset M_{0}(\mathbb{C})
and
M\subset M_{0}
. It follows that
H_{1}=H_{2}
.

Let
M_{1},\ldots,M_{k}
be the geometrically irreducible components of
M
. By
3.25
, there exists a reductive
\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho_{i}]\in M_{i}(\mathbb{C})
and
\ker\varrho_{i}=\bigcap_{[\tau]\in M_{i}(\mathbb{C})}\ker\tau
, where
\tau
ranges over all reductive representations in
M_{i}(\mathbb{C})
. Consider the product representation
\varrho:=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\prod_{i=1}^{k}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
. Then we have

\ker\varrho=\bigcap_{[\tau]\in M(\mathbb{C})}\ker\tau

where
\tau
ranges over all reductive representations in
M(\mathbb{C})
. By
3.28
, we have

\ker\varrho=H_{2}.
Therefore,
\pi_{1}(X)/H_{2}=\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
, which is a finitely generated linear group.
∎

3.4.
Construction of Shafarevich morphism (I)

Let
X
be a quasi-projective smooth variety. We will construct the Shafarevich morphism
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
associated to a Zariski closed subset
\mathfrak{C}
of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
that is invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action. Then we prove the
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is algebraic when
X
is projective.

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety. Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a Zariski closed subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
, defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, such that
\mathfrak{C}
is invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{C}
has infinite monodromy at infinity in the sense of
3.15
.
Then there exists a proper surjective holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
over a
normal complex space

{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
such that for any connected Zariski closed subset
Z
of
X
, the following properties are equivalent :

(i)

{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z)
is a point ;

(ii)

\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for any reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
;

(iii)

for any irreducible component
Z_{1}
of
Z
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for any reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.

Démonstration.

By
proposition

3.13
, there exist
reductive representations
\{\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}}_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
that underlie
\mathbb{C}
-VHS such that, for a morphism
\iota:Z\to X
from any quasi-projective
normal
variety
Z
with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\iota(Z)
being a point, the following properties hold :

(a)

For
\sigma:=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
, the image
\iota^{*}\sigma(\pi_{1}(Z))
is discrete in
\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

(b)

For each reductive
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
,
\iota^{*}\tau
is conjugate to some
\iota^{*}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
. Moreover, for each
\sigma_{i}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}
, there exists some reductive representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
such that
\iota^{*}\tau
is conjugate to
\iota^{*}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
.

(c)

We have the following inclusion :

\displaystyle\cap_{[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})}\ker\varrho\subset\ker\sigma_{i}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}}

(3.7)

where
\varrho
varies in all reductive representations such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.

Define
H:=\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho\cap\ker\sigma
, where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. By (
3.7
) we have
H=\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho
,
where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. Denote by
\widetilde{X}_{H}:=\widetilde{X}/H
. Let
\mathscr{D}
be the period domain associated with the
\mathbb{C}
-VHS
\sigma
and let
p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
be the period mapping.
We define a holomorphic map

\displaystyle\Psi:\widetilde{X}_{H}

\displaystyle\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D},

(3.8)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto(s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\pi_{H}(z),p(z))

where
\pi_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to X
denotes the covering map and
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the reduction map defined in
3.1
.

Lemma 3.30
.

Each connected component of any fiber of
\Psi
is compact.

Proof of
3.30
.

It is equivalent to prove that for any
(t,o)\in S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
, every connected component of
\Psi^{-1}(t,o)
is compact. We fix any
t\in S_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Step 1 : We first assume that each irreducible component of
(s_{\mathfrak{C}})^{-1}(t)
is normal.

Let
F
be an irreducible component of
(s_{\mathfrak{C}})^{-1}(t)
. Then the natural morphism
\iota:F\to X
is proper. By
Item

(a)
,
\Gamma:=\sigma({\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(F)}\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is a discrete subgroup of
\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

Claim 3.31
.

The period mapping
F\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
is proper.

Démonstration.

Although
F
might be singular, we can still define its period mapping since it is normal. The definition is as follows : we begin by taking a resolution of singularities
\mu:E\to F
. Since
F
is normal, each fiber of
\mu
is connected, and we have
\Gamma=\sigma({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(E)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
. It is worth noting that
\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
exists as a complex normal space since
\Gamma
is discrete. Now, consider the period mapping
E\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
for the
\mathbb{C}
-VHS induced
\mu^{*}\sigma
. This mapping then induces a holomorphic mapping
F\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
, which satisfies the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}/\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

The resulting holomorphic map
F\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
is the period mapping for the
\mathbb{C}
-VHS on
F
induced by
\sigma|_{\pi_{1}(F)}
. To establish the properness of
F\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
, it suffices to prove that
E\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
is proper. Let
\overline{X}
be a smooth projective compactification such that
D:=\overline{X}\backslash X
is a simple normal crossing divisor. Given that
E\to X
is a proper morphism, we can take a smooth projective compactification
\overline{E}
of
E
such that

—

the complement
D_{E}:=\overline{E}\setminus E
is a simple normal crossing divisor ;

—

there exists a morphism
j:\overline{E}\to\overline{X}
such that
j^{-1}(D)=D_{E}
.

We aim to prove that
j^{*}\sigma:\pi_{1}(E)\to\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has infinite monodromy at infinity.

Consider any holomorphic map
\gamma:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{E}
such that
\gamma^{-1}(D_{E})=\{0\}
. Then
(j\circ\gamma)^{-1}(D)=\{0\}
. As we assume that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
has infinite monodromy at infinity, there exists a reductive representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
and
(j\circ\gamma)^{*}\tau(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*}))
is infinite. Using
Item

(b)
, it follows that
j^{*}\tau
is conjugate to some
j^{*}\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
as
E
is smooth quasi-projective. As
\sigma^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}_{i}
is a direct factor of
\sigma
, it follows that
(j\circ\gamma)^{*}\sigma(\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*}))
is also infinite. Hence, we conclude that
j^{*}\sigma
has infinite monodromy at infinity.

By a theorem of Griffiths (cf.
[
CMP17
, Corollary 13.7.6]
), we conclude that
E\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
is proper. Therefore,
F\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
is proper.
∎

Take any point
o\in\mathscr{D}
. Note that there is a real Lie group
G_{0}
which acts holomorphically and transitively on
\mathscr{D}
. Let
V
be the compact subgroup that fixes
o
. Thus, we have
\mathscr{D}=G_{0}/V
. Now, let
Z
be any connected component of the fiber of
F\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
over
[o]
. According to
3.31
,
Z
is guaranteed to be compact. We have that
\sigma({\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)])\subset V\cap\Gamma
. Notably,
V
is compact, and
\Gamma
is discrete. As a result, it follows that
\sigma({\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Claim 3.32
.

{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]\cap H
is a finite index subgroup of
{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]
.

Démonstration.

By
Item

(b)
and (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle\ker\sigma\cap{\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)]=H\cap{\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)].

(3.9)

Since
\sigma({\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite,
\ker\sigma\cap{\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)]
is a finite index subgroup of
{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]
. The claim follows from (
3.9
).
∎

Pick any connected component
Z_{0}
of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(Z)
. Note that
{\rm Aut}(Z_{0}/Z)=\frac{{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]}{{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]\cap H}
. According to
3.32
,
{\rm Aut}(Z_{0}/Z)
is finite, implying that
Z_{0}
is compact. Hence,
\pi_{H}^{-1}(Z)
is a disjoint union of compact subvarieties of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
, each of which is a finite étale Galois cover of
Z
under
\pi_{H}
, with the Galois group
\frac{{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]}{{\rm Im}\,[{\pi_{1}(Z)}\to\pi_{1}(X)]\cap H}
. If we denote by
\widetilde{F}
a connected component of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(F)
, then each connected component of any fiber of
p|_{\widetilde{F}}:\widetilde{F}\to\mathscr{D}
is a connected component of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(Z)
, which is compact. This can be illustrated by the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}/\Gamma}

Since we have assumed that each irreducible component of
(s_{\mathfrak{C}})^{-1}(t)
is normal, it follows that for any
o\in\mathscr{D}
, each connected component of
\Psi^{-1}(t,o)
is compact.

Step 2 : we prove the general case.
In the general case, we consider an embedded resolution of singularities
\mu:Y\to X
for the fiber
(s_{\mathfrak{C}})^{-1}(t)
such that each irreducible component of
(s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\mu)^{-1}(t)
is smooth. It is worth noting that
s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\mu:Y\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
coincides with the reduction map
s_{\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}}:Y\to S_{\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}}
for
\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}(Y,N)
. Let
\widetilde{Y}_{H}:=\widetilde{X}_{H}\times_{X}Y
, which is connected.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

We observe that
\tilde{\mu}
is a proper holomorphic fibration. We define
H^{\prime}:=\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho\cap\ker\mu^{*}\sigma
, where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}
.
Since
\mu_{*}:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)
is an isomorphism, we have
(\mu_{*})^{-1}(H)=H^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\widetilde{Y}_{H}
is the covering of
Y
corresponding to
H^{\prime}
, and thus
{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{Y}_{H}/Y)=H^{\prime}\simeq H
. It is worth noting that
\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}(Y,N)\simeq M_{\rm B}(X,N)
satisfying all the conditions required for
\mathfrak{C}
as stated in
3.29
, unless the
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-invariance is not obvious. However, we note that
\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}
is invariant by
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action by
2.10
. This enables us to work with
\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}
instead of
\mathfrak{C}
.

As a result,
\mu^{*}\sigma=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\mu^{*}\sigma_{i}^{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{903653}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788563}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{788561}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle V\!H\!S$}}}}}}}
satisfies all the properties in
Items

(a)
,
(b)
and
3.7
. Note that
\mu^{*}\sigma
underlies a
\mathbb{C}
-VHS with the period mapping
p\circ\tilde{\mu}:\widetilde{Y}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
. It follows that
\Psi\circ\tilde{\mu}:\widetilde{Y}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
is defined in the same way as (
3.8
), determined by
\mu^{*}\mathfrak{C}
and
\mu^{*}\sigma
.

Therefore, by Step 1, we can conclude that for any
o\in\mathscr{D}
, each connected component of
(\Psi\circ\tilde{\mu})^{-1}(t,o)
is compact. Let
Z
be a connected component of
\Psi^{-1}(t,o)
. Then we claim that
Z
is compact. Indeed,
\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(Z)
is closed and connected as each fiber of
\tilde{\mu}
is connected. Therefore,
\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(Z)
is contained in some connected component of
(\Psi\circ\tilde{\mu})^{-1}(t,o)
. So
\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(Z)
is compact. As
\tilde{\mu}
is proper and surjective, it follows that
Z=\tilde{\mu}(\tilde{\mu}^{-1}(Z))
is compact.
3.30
is proved.
∎

As a result of
3.30
and
1.29
, the set
\widetilde{S}_{H}
of connected components of fibers of
\Psi
can be endowed with the structure of a complex normal space such that
\Psi=g\circ{\rm sh}_{H}
where
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is a proper holomorphic fibration and
g:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
is a holomorphic map. In
3.39
below, we will prove that each fiber of
g
is discrete.

Claim 3.33
.

{\rm sh}_{H}
contracts every compact subvariety of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
.

Démonstration.

Let
Z\subset\widetilde{X}_{H}
be a compact irreducible subvariety. Then,
W:=\pi_{H}(Z)
is also a compact irreducible subvariety in
X
with
\dim Z=\dim W
. Hence
{\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})]
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})
. Note that
W
can be endowed with an algebraic structure induced by
X
. As the natural map
Z\to W
is finite,
Z
can be equipped with an algebraic structure such that the natural map
Z\to X
is algebraic.

For any reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K)
with
\varrho\in\mathfrak{C}(K)
where
K
is a non archimedean local field, we have
\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])\subset\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{H})\to\pi_{1}(X)])=\{1\}
. Hence,
\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite which is thus bounded. By
3.2
,
W
is contained in a fiber of
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Consider a desingularization
Z^{\prime}
of
Z
and let
i:Z^{\prime}\to X
be the natural algebraic morphism. Note that
i^{*}\sigma(\pi_{1}(Z^{\prime}))=\{1\}
. It follows that the variation of Hodge structure induced by
i^{*}\sigma
is trivial. Therefore,
p(Z)
is a point. Hence
Z
is contracted by
\Psi
. The claim follows.
∎

Lemma 3.34
.

There is an action of
{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{X}_{H}/X)=\pi_{1}(X)/H
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
that is equivariant for the proper holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
. This action is analytic and properly discontinuous. Namely, for any point
y

of
\widetilde{S}_{H}
, there exists an open neighborhood
V_{y}
of
y
such that the set

\{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)/H\mid\gamma.V_{y}\cap V_{y}\neq\varnothing\}

is finite.

Démonstration.

Take any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)/H
. We can consider
\gamma
as an analytic automorphism of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
. According to
3.33
,
{\rm sh}_{H}\circ\gamma:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
contracts each fiber of the proper holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
. As a result, it induces a holomorphic map
\tilde{\gamma}:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
such that we have the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\gamma}}

Let us define the action of
\gamma
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
by
\tilde{\gamma}
. Then
\gamma
is an analytic automorphism and
{\rm sh}_{H}
is
\pi_{1}(X)/H
-equivariant. It is evident that
\tilde{\gamma}:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
carries one fiber of
{\rm sh}_{H}
to another fiber. Thus, we have shown that
\pi_{1}(X)/H
acts on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
analytically and equivariantly with respect to
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
. Now, we will prove that this action is properly discontinuous.

Take any
y\in\widetilde{S}_{H}
and let
F:={\rm sh}_{H}^{-1}(y)
. Consider the subgroup
\mathcal{S}
of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
that fixes
y
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}:=\{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)/H\mid\gamma\cdot F=F\}.

(3.10)

Since
F
is compact,
\mathcal{S}
is finite.

Claim 3.35
.

F
is a connected component of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(\pi_{H}(F))
.

Proof of
3.35
.

Let
x\in\pi_{H}^{-1}(\pi_{H}(F))
. Then there exists
x_{0}\in F
such that
\pi_{H}(x)=\pi_{H}(x_{0})
. Therefore, there exists
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)/H
such that
\gamma.x_{0}=x
. It follows that
\pi_{H}^{-1}(\pi_{H}(F))=\cup_{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)/H}\gamma.F
. Since
\gamma
carries one fiber of
{\rm Sh}_{H}
to another fiber, and the group
\pi_{1}(X)/H
is finitely presented, it follows that
\cup_{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)/H}\gamma.F
are countable union of fibers of
{\rm Sh}_{H}
. It follows that
F
is a connected component of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(\pi_{H}(F))
.
∎

3.35
implies that
\pi_{H}:F\to\pi_{H}(F)
is a finite étale cover. Denote by
Z:=\pi_{H}(F)
which is a connected Zariski closed subset of
X
. Then
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(Z)]
is finite. As a consequence of
[
Hof09
, Theorem 4.5]
, there is a connected open neighborhood
U
of
Z
such that
\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(U)
is an isomorphism. Therefore,
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(U)\to\pi_{1}(W)]={\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(W)]
is also finite. As a result,
\pi_{H}^{-1}(U)
is a disjoint union of connected open sets
\{U_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
such that

(a)

For each
U_{\alpha}
,
\pi_{H}|_{U_{\alpha}}:U_{\alpha}\to U
is a finite étale covering.

(b)

Each
U_{\alpha}
contains exactly one connected component of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(Z)
.

We may assume that
F\subset U_{\alpha_{1}}
for some
\alpha_{1}\in I
. By
Item

(b)
, for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X,z)/H\simeq{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{X}_{H}/X)
,
\gamma\cdot U_{\alpha_{1}}\cap U_{\alpha_{1}}=\varnothing
if and only if
\gamma\notin\mathcal{S}
.

Since
{\rm sh}_{H}
is a proper holomorphic fibration, we can take a neighborhood
V_{y}
of
y
such that
{\rm sh}_{H}^{-1}(V_{y})\subset U_{\alpha_{1}}
. Since
\gamma\cdot U_{\alpha_{1}}\cap U_{\alpha_{1}}=\varnothing
if and only if
\gamma\notin\mathcal{S}
, it follows that
\gamma\cdot V_{y}\cap V_{y}=\varnothing
if
\gamma\not\in\mathcal{S}
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is finite and
y
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that the action of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
is properly discontinuous. Thus
3.34
is proven.
∎

Let
\nu:\pi_{1}(X)/H\to{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{S}_{H})
be action of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
and let
\Gamma_{0}:=\nu(\pi_{1}(X)/H)
. By
3.34
and
[
Car60
]
, we know that the quotient
{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X):=\widetilde{S}_{H}/\Gamma_{0}
is a complex normal space, and it is compact if
X
is compact. Moreover, since
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is
\nu
-equivariant, it induces a proper holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
from
X
to a complex normal space
{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

(3.11)

Claim 3.36
.

For any connected Zariski closed subset
Z\subset X
, the following properties are equivalent :

(a)

{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z)
is a point ;

(b)

\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for any reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
;

(c)

for any irreducible component
Z_{1}
of
Z
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for any reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.

Démonstration.

(c)

\Rightarrow

(a) : Let
f:Y\to Z_{1}
be a desingularization. For any non archimedean local field
K
, we note that there exists an abstract embedding
K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Hence, for any reductive representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K)
with
[\rho]\in\mathfrak{C}(K)
,
f^{*}\rho(\pi_{1}(Y))\subset\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite, and therefore bounded. Hence,
f(Y)
is contained in some fiber
F
of
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
by

3.2
. Using
Items

(a)
and
(b)
, we have that
\Gamma:=f^{*}\sigma(\pi_{1}(Y))
is also finite. Therefore,
Y
is mapped to one point by the period mapping
Y\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
of
f^{*}\sigma
. As a result,
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z_{1})
is a point by (
). Since
Z
is connected,
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z)
is also a point.

(b)

\Rightarrow

(c) : Obvious.

(a)

\Rightarrow

(b) : We observe from (
) that for any connected component
Z^{\prime}
of
\pi_{H}^{-1}(Z)
, it is contracted by
\Psi
. By
3.30
,
Z^{\prime}
is contained in some compact subvariety of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
. Since
Z^{\prime}
is closed, it is also compact. Therefore, the map
Z^{\prime}\to Z
induced by
\pi_{H}
is a finite étale cover.

Let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be any reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. Note that
\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z^{\prime})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is a finite index subgroup of
\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
. Since
\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z^{\prime})\to\pi_{1}(X)])=\{1\}
, it follows that
\varrho({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite. The claim is proved.
∎

Therefore, we have constructed the desired proper holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
. The theorem is proved.
∎

Proposition 3.37
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and let
\mathfrak{C}
be a constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
which is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and is invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action. Then
{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
is a projective variety and
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is algebraic.

Démonstration.

We will use the same notations as in the proof of
3.29
.

Claim 3.38
.

There exists a finite index normal subgroup
N
of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
such that its action on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
does not have fixed point.

Proof of
3.38
.

By
3.28
, we know that
\pi_{1}(X)/H
is a finitely presented linear group. Therefore, by Selberg’s lemma it contains a finite index normal subgroup
N
which is torsion free.
By (
3.10
), we know that for any
y\in\widetilde{S}_{H}
, the subgroup of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
fixing
y
is finite. It follows that the action of
N
on
y\in\widetilde{S}_{H}
is torsion free.
∎

Let
Y:=\widetilde{X}_{H}/N
, where
N
is the finite index torsion free normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
. Then
Y\to X
is a finite Galois étale cover with
{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{X}_{H}/Y)=N
. Recall that we define
