\nu:\pi_{1}(X)/H\to{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{S}_{H})
to be action of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
. Since
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is
\nu
-equivariant, the group
N
gives rise to a proper holomorphic fibration

Y\to\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
over a complex normal space
\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
. By
3.31
and
3.38
,
\nu(N)
acts on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
properly continuous and freely and thus the covering
\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
is étale.

Claim 3.39
.

Each fiber of
g:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
is discrete.

Démonstration.

Let
(t,o)\in S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
be arbitrary point and take any point
y\in g^{-1}((t,o))
. Then
Z:={\rm sh}_{H}^{-1}(y)
is a connected component of the fiber
\Psi^{-1}((t,o))
, that is compact by
3.30
. By
1.29
,
Z
has an open neighborhood
U
such that
\Psi(U)
is a locally closed analytic subvariety of
S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
and
\Psi|_{U}:U\rightarrow\Psi(U)
is proper. Therefore, for the Stein factorization
U\to V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{V}}}{{\to}}\Psi(U)
of
\Psi|_{U}
,
U\to V
coincides with
{\rm sh}_{H}|_{U}:U\to{\rm sh}_{H}(U)
and
\pi_{V}:V\to\Psi(U)
is finite. Observe that
V
is an open neighborhood of
y
and
\pi_{V}:V\to\Psi(U)
coincides with
g|_{V}:V\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
. Therefore, the set
V\cap g^{-1}((t,o))=V\cap(\pi_{V})^{-1}(t,o)
is finite. As a result,
g^{-1}((t,o))
is discrete. The claim is proven.
∎

In
[
Gri70
]
, Griffiths discovered a so-called
canonical bundle
K_{\mathscr{D}}
on the period domain
\mathscr{D}
, which is invariant under
G_{0}
. Here
G_{0}
is a real Lie group acting on
\mathscr{D}
holomorphically and transitively. It is worth noting that
K_{\mathscr{D}}
is endowed with a
G_{0}
-invariant smooth metric
h_{\mathscr{D}}
whose curvature is positive-definite in the horizontal direction. The period mapping
p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
induces a holomorphic map
\phi:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
which is horizontal. It is important to note that the monodromy representation
\sigma:\pi_{1}(X)/H\to G_{0}
induces a representation
\nu(\pi_{1}(X)/H)\to G_{0}
, such that
\phi
is equivariant with respect to this representation. As a result,
\phi^{*}K_{\mathscr{D}}
descends to a line bundle on the quotient
W:=\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
, denoted by
L_{G}
.
The smooth metric
h_{\mathscr{D}}
induces a smooth metric
h_{G}
on
L_{G}
whose curvature form is denoted by
T
. Let
x\in\widetilde{S}_{H}
be a smooth point of
\widetilde{S}_{H}
and let
v\in T_{\widetilde{S}_{H},x}
. Then
-iT(v,\bar{v})>0
if
d\phi(v)\neq 0
.

Claim 3.40
.

{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
is a projective normal variety.

Démonstration.

Note that
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
is a projective normal variety. We take an ample line bundle
L
over
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Recall that there is a line bundle
L_{\rm G}
on
W
equipped with a smooth metric
h_{\rm G}
such that its curvature form is
T
. Denote by
f:W\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
the natural morphism induced by
g:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
. Let
\mu:W^{\prime}\to W
be a resolution of singularities of
W
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

We take a smooth metric
h
on
L
such that its curvature form
i\Theta_{h}(L)
is Kähler. As shown in
3.39
, the map
g:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
is discrete. Therefore,
g
is an immersion at general points of
\widetilde{S}_{H}
. Thus, for the line bundle
\mu^{*}(L_{\rm G}\otimes f^{*}L)
equipped with the smooth metric
\mu^{*}(h\otimes f^{*}h_{\rm G})
, its curvature form is strictly positive at some points of
W^{\prime}
. By Demailly’s holomorphic Morse inequality or Siu’s solution for the Grauert-Riemenschneider conjecture,
\mu^{*}(L_{\rm G}\otimes f^{*}L)
is a big line bundle and thus
W^{\prime}
is a Moishezon manifold. Hence
W
is a Moishezon variety.

Moreover, we can verify that for irreducible positive-dimensional closed subvariety
Z
of
W
, there exists a smooth point
x
in
Z
such that it has a neighborhood
\Omega
that can be lifted to the étale covering
\widetilde{S}_{H}
of
W
, and
g|_{\Omega}:\Omega\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
is an immersion.
It follows that

(if^{*}\Theta_{h}(L)+T)|_{\Omega}
is strictly positive.
Since
if^{*}\Theta_{h}(L)+T\geq 0
, it follows that

(L_{G}\otimes f^{*}L)^{\dim Z}\cdot[Z]=\int_{Z^{\rm reg}}(if^{*}\Theta_{h}(L)+T)^{\dim Z}>0.

By the Nakai-Moishezon criterion for Moishezon varieties (cf.
[
Kol90
, Theorem 3.11]
),
L_{G}\otimes f^{*}L
is ample, implying that
W
is projective.
Recall that the compact complex normal space
{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X):=\widetilde{S}_{H}/\Gamma_{0}
is a quotient of
W=\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
by the finite group
\Gamma_{0}/\nu(N)
. Therefore,
{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
is also projective. The claim is proved.
∎

We accomplish the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 3.41
.

In
3.29
, when
X
is compact,
proposition

3.13
allows us to assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is constructible rather than Zariski closed. Under this weaker assumption, we can still obtain the Shafarevich morphism for
\mathfrak{C}
.

Remark 3.42
.

We remark that
3.34
is claimed without a proof in
[
Eys04
, p. 524]
and
[
Bru23
, Proof of Theorem 10]
. It appears to us that the proof of
3.34
is not straightforward.

It is worth noting that
3.38
is implicitly used in
[
Eys04
, Proposition 5.3.10]
. In that proof, the criterion for Stein spaces (cf.
Proposition

1.14
) is employed, assuming
3.38
.
Given its significance in the proofs of
C
and
D
, we provide a complete proof.

3.5.
Construction of Shafarevich morphism (II)

In the previous subsection, we established the existence of the Shafarevich morphism associated with a constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
that are invariant under
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-action. In this section, we focus on proving an existence theorem for the Shafarevich morphism associated with a single reductive representation, based on
3.29
.
Initially, we assume that the representation has infinite monodromy at infinity and that
X
is smooth. However, we will subsequently apply
proposition

3.19
to remove this assumption and establish the more general result.

Proposition 3.43
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety. Let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation with infinite monodromy at infinity.
Then there exists a proper holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
onto a complex normal space
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
such that for any Zariski closed subvariety
Z\subset X
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite if and only if
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(Z)
is a point. If
X
is smooth, then for any Zariski closed subset
Z\subset X
, the following properties are equivalent :

(i)

{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(Z)
is a point ;

(ii)

\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite ;

(iii)

for each irreducible component
Z_{1}
of
Z
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

We first prove the following crucial result.

Proposition 3.44
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety. Let
f:Z\to X
be a
proper
morphism from a
smooth
quasi-projective variety
Z
. Let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation. Define
M:=j_{Z}^{-1}\{1\}
, where
1
stands for the trivial representation, and
j_{Z}:M_{\rm B}(X,N)\to M_{\rm B}(Z,N)
is the natural morphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme induced by
f
.
Then
M
is a closed subscheme of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
such that
M(\mathbb{C})
is invariant under
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.

Démonstration.

We take a smooth projective compactification
\overline{X}
(resp.
\overline{Z}
) of
X
(resp.
Z
) such that
D:=\overline{X}\backslash X
(resp.
D_{Z}:=\overline{Z}\backslash Z
) is a simple normal crossing divisor and
f
extends to a morphism
\bar{f}:\overline{X}\to\overline{Z}
. Note that the morphism
j_{Z}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-morphism between affine schemes of finite type
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
and
M_{\rm B}(Z,N)
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. We remark that
M
is a closed subscheme of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
as we have
\{1\}\in M_{\rm B}(Z,N)(\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in M(\mathbb{C})
. By
1.6
, there is a tame pure imaginary harmonic bundle
(E,\theta,h)
on
X
such that
\varrho
is the monodromy representation of
\nabla_{h}+\theta+\theta_{h}^{\dagger}
.
By definition of
M
,
f^{*}\varrho
is a trivial representation. Therefore,
f^{*}\varrho
corresponds to a trivial harmonic bundle
(\oplus^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z},0,h_{0})
where
h_{0}
is the canonical metric for the trivial vector bundle
\oplus^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
with zero curvature. By the unicity theorem in
[
Moc06
, Theorem 1.4]
,
(\oplus^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z},0,h_{0})
coincides with
(f^{*}E,f^{*}\theta,f^{*}h)
with some obvious ambiguity of
h_{0}
. Therefore,
f^{*}E=\oplus^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
and
f^{*}\theta=0
. In particular, the regular filtered Higgs bundle
(\tilde{E}_{*},\tilde{\theta})
on
(\overline{Z},D_{Z})
induced by the prolongation of
(f^{*}E,f^{*}\theta,f^{*}h)
using norm growth defined in
section

2.1
is trivial ; namely we have
{}_{\bm{a}}\tilde{E}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Z}}^{N}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Z}}(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}a_{i}D^{\prime}_{i})
for any
\bm{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
\tilde{\theta}=0
. Here we write
D_{Z}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}D^{\prime}_{i}
.

Let
(\bm{E}_{*},\theta)
be the induced regular filtered Higgs bundle on
(\overline{X},D)
by
(E,\theta,h)
defined in
section

2.1
. According to
sections

2.2
and
2.3
we can define the pullback
(f^{*}\bm{E}_{*},f^{*}\theta)
, which also forms a regular filtered Higgs bundle on
(\overline{Z},D_{Z})
with trivial characteristic numbers. By virtue of
proposition

2.5
, we deduce that
(f^{*}\bm{E}_{*},f^{*}\theta)=(\tilde{E}_{*},\tilde{\theta})
. Consequently, it follows that
(f^{*}\bm{E}_{*},f^{*}\theta)
is trivial. Hence
(f^{*}\bm{E}_{*},tf^{*}\theta)
is trivial for any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

Fix some ample line bundle
L
on
\overline{Z}
. It is worth noting that for any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
(\bm{E}_{*},t\theta)
is
\mu_{L}
-polystable with trivial characteristic numbers. By
[
Moc06
, Theorem 9.4]
, there is a pluriharmonic metric
h_{t}
for
(E,t\theta)
adapted to the parabolic structures of
(\bm{E}_{*},t\theta)
. By
proposition

2.5
once again, the regular filtered Higgs bundle
(f^{*}\bm{E}_{*},tf^{*}\theta)
is the prolongation of the tame harmonic bundle
(f^{*}E,tf^{*}\theta,f^{*}h_{t})
using norm growth defined in
section

2.1
. Since
(f^{*}\bm{E}_{*},tf^{*}\theta)
is trivial for any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, by the unicity theorem in
[
Moc06
, Theorem 1.4]
once again, it follows that
(\oplus^{N}\mathcal{O}_{Z},0,h_{0})
coincides with
(f^{*}E,tf^{*}\theta,f^{*}h_{t})
with some obvious ambiguity of
h_{0}
. Recall that in
section

2.4
,
\varrho_{t}
is defined to be the monodromy representation of the flat connection
\nabla_{h_{t}}+t\theta+\bar{t}\theta_{h_{t}}^{\dagger}
. It follows that
f^{*}\varrho_{t}
is the monodromy representation of the flat connection
f^{*}(\nabla_{h_{t}}+t\theta+\bar{t}\theta_{h_{t}}^{\dagger})
. Therefore,
f^{*}\varrho_{t}
is a trivial representation.

However, it is worth noting that
\varrho_{t}
might not be reductive as
(E,t\theta,h_{t})
might not be pure imaginary. Let
\varrho_{t}^{ss}
be the semisimplification of
\varrho_{t}
. Then
[\varrho_{t}]=[\varrho_{t}^{ss}]
. Since
f^{*}\varrho_{t}
is a trivial representation,
f^{*}\varrho_{t}^{ss}
is also trivial. Note that

f^{*}(t.[\varrho])=f^{*}[\varrho_{t}]=f^{*}[\varrho_{t}^{ss}]=[f^{*}\varrho_{t}^{ss}]=1.

Therefore,
t.[\varrho]\in M(\mathbb{C})
if
[\varrho]\in M(\mathbb{C})
. The proposition is proved.
∎

Remark 3.45
.

It is important to note that, unlike the projective case, the proof of
proposition

3.44
becomes considerably non-trivial when
X
is quasi-projective. This complexity arises from the utilization of the functoriality of pullback of regular filtered Higgs bundles, which is established in
proposition

2.5
. Lemma
3.44
plays a crucial role in the proof of
proposition

3.43
as it allows us to remove the condition of
\mathbb{R}^{*}
-invariance in
3.29
. However, we remark that
proposition

3.44
is claimed without a proof in the proof of
[
Bru23
, Lemma 9.3]
.

Proof of
proposition

3.43
.

Step 1 : we assume that
X
is smooth, and
\varrho
has infinite monodromy at infinity.
Let
f:Z\to X
be a
proper
morphism from a
smooth
quasi-projective variety
Z
. Then
j_{Z}:M_{\rm B}(X,N)\to M_{\rm B}(Z,N)
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme. Define

\mathfrak{C}:=\bigcap_{\{f:Z\to X\mid f^{*}\varrho=1\}}j_{Z}^{-1}\{1\},

(3.12)

where
1
stands for the trivial representation, and
f:Z\to X
ranges over all proper morphisms from smooth quasi-projective varieties
Z
to
X
.
Then
\mathfrak{C}
is a Zariski closed subset defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, and by
proposition

3.44
,
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
is invariant under
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.
Note that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.
As we assume that
\varrho
has infinite monodromy at infinity, conditions in
3.29
for
\mathfrak{C}
are fulfilled.
Therefore, we apply
3.29
to conclude that the Shafarevich morphism
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
exists. It is a proper holomorphic fibration over a complex normal space.

Claim 3.46
.

For any connected Zariski closed subset
Z\subset X
, the following properties are equivalent :

(a)

{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z)
is a point ;

(b)

\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite ;

(c)

for each irreducible component
Z_{1}
of
Z
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Démonstration.

(a)

\Rightarrow

(b) : this follows from the fact that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
and
3.29
.

(b)

\Rightarrow

(c) : obvious.

(c)

\Rightarrow

(a) : Consider the desingularization
Y\to Z_{1}
, and let
f:Y\to X
be the composite morphism.
Since
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})
is surjective, it follows that
f^{*}\varrho(\pi_{1}(Y))
is finite. We can take a finite étale cover
W\to Y
such that
f^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W)\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is trivial. Denote by
g:W\to X
the composition of
f
with
W\to Y
. Then
g
is proper and
g^{*}\varrho=1
.
Let
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be any reductive representation such that
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then
g^{*}\tau=1
by (
). It follows that
f^{*}\tau(\pi_{1}(Y))
is finite.
According to
3.29
,
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ f(Y)={\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z_{1})
is a point. Since
Z
is connected,
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(Z)
is a point.
∎

Let
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
be
{\rm sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\mathfrak{C}}(X)
. The proposition is proved.
∎

The conditions in
proposition

3.43
that
\varrho
has infinite monodromy at infinity and that
X
is smooth pose significant practical limitations for further applications. However, we will remove this assumption in next theorem. We first prove an important lemma.

Lemma 3.47
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper surjective morphism between connected (possible reducible) quasi-projective varieties
X
and
Y
. Assume that each fiber of
f
is connected. Then
f_{*}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(Y)
is surjective.

Démonstration.

Let
\widetilde{Y}\to Y
be the universal covering of
Y
. Consider the fiber product
X^{\prime}:=X\times_{Y}\widetilde{Y}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Since
f
is proper and each fiber of
f
is connected, it follows that
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to\widetilde{Y}
is proper, surjective and each fiber of
f^{\prime}
is connected.

Claim 3.48
.

X^{\prime}
is connected.

Démonstration.

Assume by contradiction that
X^{\prime}
is not connected. Then
X^{\prime}=\sqcup_{\alpha\in I}X_{\alpha}
, where
X_{\alpha}
are connected components of
X^{\prime}
. Since each fiber of
f^{\prime}
is connected, it follows that any fiber of
f^{\prime}
is contained in some
X_{\alpha}
. This implies that
f^{\prime}(X_{\alpha})\cap f^{\prime}(X_{\beta})=\varnothing
if
\alpha\neq\beta
. Since
f^{\prime}
is surjective, it follows
\sqcup_{\alpha\in I}f^{\prime}(X_{\alpha})=\widetilde{Y}
. Note that
f^{\prime}(X_{\alpha})
and
f^{\prime}(\sqcup_{\beta\in I,\beta\neq\alpha}X_{\beta})
are both closed since
f^{\prime}
is proper. This contradicts with the connectedness of
\widetilde{Y}
. Hence
X^{\prime}
is connected.
∎

We choose a base point
x\in X
and
y\in Y
such that
y=f(x)
. Let
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}\in\widetilde{Y}
be such that
x=\pi_{1}(x^{\prime})
,
y=\pi_{2}(y^{\prime})
and
y^{\prime}=f^{\prime}(x^{\prime})
. Then for any element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(Y,y)
, the lift of
\gamma
in
\widetilde{Y}
starting at
y^{\prime}
will end at some
y^{\prime\prime}
such that
y=\pi_{2}(y^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
X^{\prime}=X\times_{Y}\widetilde{Y}
, it follows that there exists a unique
x^{\prime\prime}\in f^{\prime-1}(y^{\prime\prime})
such that
x=\pi_{1}(x^{\prime\prime})
.
Since
X^{\prime}
is connected, there exists a continuous path
\sigma:[0,1]\to X^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}=\sigma(0)
and
x^{\prime\prime}=\sigma(1)
. Consider the continuous path
f^{\prime}\circ\sigma:[0,1]\to\widetilde{Y}
. Then
y^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\circ\sigma(0)
and
y^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime}\circ\sigma(1)
. It follows that
\gamma=[\pi_{2}\circ f^{\prime}\circ\sigma]=[f\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma]
. Note that
x=\pi_{1}\circ\sigma(0)=\pi_{1}\circ\sigma(1)
. This proves that
f_{*}([\pi_{1}\circ\sigma])=\gamma
. Therefore,
f_{*}:\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\pi_{1}(Y,y)
is surjective. The lemma is proved.
∎

Theorem 3.49
.

Let
X
be a quasi-projective normal varieties, and let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation.
Then there exists a dominant holomorphic map
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
to a complex normal space
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
whose general fibers are connected such that for any Zariski closed subset

Z\subset X
, the following properties are equivalent :

(a)

{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(Z)
is a point ;

(b)

\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite ;

(c)

for any irreducible component
Z_{1}
of
Z
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Démonstration.

Step 1 : We first assume that
X
is smooth.

By
proposition

3.19
, there exists a smooth partial compactification
X^{\prime}
of
X
such that
\varrho
has quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity with respect to
X^{\prime}
.

Claim 3.50
.

There exists a finite morphism
\nu^{\prime}:\widehat{X}^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
such that

(1)

\widehat{X}^{\prime}
is a quasi-projective smooth variety ;

(2)

We denote
\nu_{0}:\widehat{X}\to X
, where
\widehat{X}=(\nu^{\prime})^{-1}(X)
.
Then
\nu_{0}^{*}\varrho
extends to a reductive representation
\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}^{\prime})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
that has infinite monodromy at infinity.

Démonstration.

Let
D_{1}+\cdots+D_{l}=X^{\prime}-X
be the irreducible decomposition of the boundary, which is simple normal crossing.
Let
m_{1},\ldots,m_{l}
be the orders of the local monodromy around
D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}
.
By Kawamata’s covering lemma (see
[
Kaw81
, Theorem 17]
), we may take a finite covering
\nu^{\prime}:\widehat{X}^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
such that

(1)

\hat{X}^{\prime}
is smooth,

(2)

(\nu^{\prime})^{-1}(D_{1}+\cdots+D_{l})
is simple normal crossing,

(3)

let
(\nu^{\prime})^{*}D_{i}=\sum n_{ij}F_{j}
be the irreducible decomposition, then
m_{i}|n_{ij}
for any
i
and
j
.

Then
\nu_{0}^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(\hat{X})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has trivial monodromy at each boundary components
F_{ij}
.
Hence this extends to
\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}^{\prime})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, which has infinite monodromy at infinity.
∎

We proceed by finding a finite morphism
h:Y^{\prime}\to\widehat{X}^{\prime}
from a normal quasi-projective variety
Y^{\prime}
such that the composition
f:Y^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
of
\widehat{X}^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}\to\widehat{X}^{\prime}
is a Galois cover with Galois group
G
(cf.
[
CDY22
, §1.3]
). By
3.50
and
3.17
,
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
also has infinite monodromy at infinity. Consequently, we can apply
proposition

3.43
to deduce the existence of a proper holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}:Y^{\prime}\to{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
such that for any closed subvariety
Z
of
Y^{\prime}
,
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Z)
is a point if and only if
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})])
is finite.

Claim 3.51
.

The Galois group
G
acts analytically on
{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
such that
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}
is
G
-equivariant.

Démonstration.

Take any
y\in{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
and any
g\in G
. Since
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}
is surjective and proper, the fiber
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}(y)
is thus non-empty and compact. Let
Z
be an irreducible component of the fiber
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}(y)
.
Then
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})])
is finite, implying that
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}((g.Z)^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})])
is also finite. Consequently, there exists a point
y^{\prime}\in{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
such that
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(g.Z)=y^{\prime}
. Since each fiber of
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}
is connected, for any other irreducible component
Z^{\prime}
of
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}^{-1}(y)
, we have
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(g.Z^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
. Consequently, it follows that
g
maps each fiber of
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}
to another fiber.

We consider
g
as an analytic automorphism of
Y^{\prime}
. For the holomorphic map
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}\circ g:Y^{\prime}\to{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
, since it contracts each fiber of
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}:Y^{\prime}\to{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
to a point, it induces a holomorphic map
\tilde{g}:{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})\to{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
such that we have the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{g}}

(3.13)

Let us define the holomorphic map
\tilde{g}:{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})\to{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
to be the action of
g\in G
on
{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
. Based on (
), it is clear that
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}
is
G
-equivariant. Therefore, the claim is proven.
∎

Note that
X^{\prime}:=Y^{\prime}/G
. The quotient of
{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})
by
G
, resulting in a complex normal space denoted by
Q
(cf.
[
Car60
]
).
Then
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}
induces a proper holomorphic fibration

c^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to Q.

Consider the restriction
c:=c^{\prime}|_{X}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}

(3.14)

Claim 3.52
.

For any closed subvariety
Z
of
X
,
c(Z)
is a point if and only if
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})])
is finite.

Démonstration.

Let
Y:=f^{-1}(X)
and
f_{0}:=f|_{Y}
. Note that
f_{0}:Y\to X
is a Galois cover with Galois group
G
. We have
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}|_{\pi_{1}(Y)}=f_{0}^{*}\varrho
. Now, consider any closed subvariety
Z
of
X
. There exists an irreducible closed subvariety
W
of
Y
such that
f_{0}(W)=Z
. Let
\overline{W}
be the closure of
W
in
Y^{\prime}
, which is an irreducible closed subvariety of
Y^{\prime}
.

Observe that
c(Z)
is a point if and only if
{\rm sh}_{h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}}(\overline{W})
is a point, which is equivalent to
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(\overline{W}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime})])
being finite by
Proposition

3.43
. Furthermore, this is equivalent to
f_{0}^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}({W}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
being finite since
h^{*}\varrho^{\prime}|_{\pi_{1}(Y)}=f_{0}^{*}\varrho
. Since
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})]
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})
, the above condition is equivalent to
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}({Z}^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
being finite.
∎

Let
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:=c
and
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X):=Q
. This is our construction of the Shafarevich morphism of
\varrho
.

Step 2 : We does not assume that
X
is smooth.
We take a desingularization
\nu_{1}:X_{1}\to X
. Then
\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(X_{1})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is also a reductive representation. Based on Step 1, the Shafarevich morphism
{\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}:X_{1}\to{\rm Sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(X_{1})
exists. Let
Z
be an irreducible component of a fiber of
\nu_{1}
. Then
\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho(\pi_{1}(Z))=\{1\}
. It follows that
{\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(Z)
is a point. Note that each fiber of
\nu_{1}
is connected as
X
is normal. It follows that each fiber of
\nu_{1}
is contracted to a point by
{\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}
. Therefore, by the universal property of the Stein factorization, there exists a dominant holomorphic map
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(X_{1})
with connected general fibers such that we have the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(X_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{\varrho}}

(3.15)

Claim 3.53
.

For any closed subvariety
Z\subset X
,
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(Z)
is a point if and only if
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Démonstration.

Let us choose an irreducible component
W
of
\nu_{1}^{-1}(Z)
which is surjective onto
Z
. Since
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})]
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})
, and
(\nu_{1})_{*}:\pi_{1}(X_{1})\to\pi_{1}(X)
is surjective, it follows that
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite if and only if
\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})])
is finite.

Proof of
\Rightarrow
:
Note that
{\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(W)
is a point and thus
\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is finite by
3.52
. Hence
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Proof of
\Leftarrow
:
Note that
\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})])
is finite.
Therefore,
{\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(W)
is a point and thus

{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(Z)={\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ\nu_{1}(W)={\rm sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(W)

is a point by
3.52
.
∎

Let us write
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X):={\rm Sh}_{\nu_{1}^{*}\varrho}(X_{1})
. Then
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is the construction of the Shafarevich morphism associated with
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

Step 3. We prove the last assertion on the three equivalent properties for
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}
.

Note that the equivalence between
Item

(c)
and
Item

(a)
is proved in
3.53
. The implication of
Item

(b)
to
Item

(c)
is obvious.
We thus only need to prove that
Item

(a)
to
Item

(b)
.
Let
Z\subset X
be a connected Zariski closed subset.

We first prove the following claim.

Claim 3.54
.

Let
g:Y\to X
be a dominant morphism between normal quasi-projective varieties and
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation. Then for every connected Zariski closed set
Z\subset Y
,
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ g(Z)
is a point if and only if
{\rm sh}_{g^{*}\varrho}(Z)
is a point.

Proof of
3.54
.

It is enough to prove the case that
Z
is irreducible.
Hence we need to show that for every closed subvariety
Z\subset Y
,
g^{*}\varrho(\mathrm{Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is finite if and only if
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ g(Z)
is a point.
So first suppose
g^{*}\varrho(\mathrm{Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is finite.
Let
V\subset X
be the Zariski closure of
g(Z)
.
Then the induced map
Z\to V
is dominant, hence induces
Z^{\mathrm{norm}}\to V^{\mathrm{norm}}
.
Then
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(V^{\mathrm{norm}})]
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(V^{\mathrm{norm}})
.
Hence
\varrho(\mathrm{Im}[\pi_{1}(V^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.
This shows
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(V)
is a point.
Hence
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ g(Z)
is a point.
Conversely, assume
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ g(Z)
is a point.
Then
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}(V)
is a point.
Thus
\varrho(\mathrm{Im}[\pi_{1}(V^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.
Hence
g^{*}\varrho(\mathrm{Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is finite.
∎

Step 3-1.
Item

(a)

\Rightarrow

Item

(b)
when
X
is smooth.

Since
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is residually finite by Malcev’s theorem, we can find a finite étale cover
\nu:\widehat{X}\to X
such that
\nu^{*}\varrho(\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}))
is torsion free.
By
proposition

3.19
, there exists a partial smooth compactification
\widehat{X}^{\prime}
such that
\nu^{*}\varrho
extends to a reductive representation
\sigma:\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}^{\prime})\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with infinite monodromy at infinity.
Let
\widehat{Z}\subset\widehat{X}
be a connected component of
\nu^{-1}(Z)
.
Then
\widehat{Z}\to Z
is finite étale.
Let
\widehat{Z}^{\prime}\subset\widehat{X}^{\prime}
be the Zariski closure.
Then
\widehat{Z}^{\prime}
is connected.

Now by
3.54
applied to
\nu:\widehat{X}\to X
, we conclude that
\mathrm{sh}_{\nu^{*}\varrho}(\widehat{Z})
is a point.
Hence by
3.54
applied to
\iota:\widehat{X}\hookrightarrow\widehat{X}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\mathrm{sh}_{\sigma}(\widehat{Z})
is a point.
Hence
\mathrm{sh}_{\sigma}(\widehat{Z}^{\prime})
is a point.
Thus by
proposition

3.43
,
\sigma({\rm}[\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z}^{\prime})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}^{\prime})])
is finite.
Note that

\displaystyle\nu^{*}\varrho({\rm}[\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X})])=\iota^{*}\sigma({\rm}[\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X})])

\displaystyle=\sigma({\rm}[\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}^{\prime})])\subset\sigma({\rm}[\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z}^{\prime})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X}^{\prime})]).

It follows that
\nu^{*}\varrho({\rm}[\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z})\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X})])
is finite.
Since the image
\pi_{1}(\widehat{Z})\to\pi_{1}(Z)
has finite index,
\varrho({\rm}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Step 3-2.
Item

(a)

\Rightarrow

Item

(b)
in the general case.

Let
\nu:X_{1}\to X
be a desingularization.
Let
Z_{1}:=\nu^{-1}(Z)
, which might be reducible.
Since
X
is normal, each fiber of
\nu
is connected.
Hence
Z_{1}
is connected, and
\nu|_{Z_{1}}:Z_{1}\to Z
is a proper surjective morphism between connected (possibly reducible) quasi-projective varieties such that each fiber is connected.
By
3.47
, we have the surjectivity of
\pi_{1}(Z_{1})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(Z)
.
Hence
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite if and only if
\nu^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})])
is finite.
By (
),
{\rm sh}_{\nu^{*}\varrho}={\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ\nu
.
It then follows that
{\rm sh}_{\nu^{*}\varrho}(Z_{1})
is a point.
Hence by step 3-1,
\nu^{*}\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{1})\to\pi_{1}(X_{1})])
is finite.
Hence
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.
The theorem is proved.
∎

3.6.
On the algebraicity of the Shafarevich morphism

In
3.49
, when
X
is compact, we proved that the image
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is projective. In general, as mentioned in
0.3
, we propose the following conjecture.

Conjecture 3.55

(Algebraicity of Shafarevich morphism)
.

Let
X
,
\varrho
and
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
be as in
3.49
. Then
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is a quasi-projective normal variety and
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is an algebraic morphism.

This conjecture seems to be a difficult problem, with the special case when
\varrho
arises from a
\mathbb{Z}
-VHS known as a long-standing Griffiths conjecture. In this paper, we provide confirmation of such expectations at the function field level.

We first recall the definition of
(bi)meromorphic maps
of complex spaces
X
and
Y
in the sense of Remmert.

Definition 3.56

(Meromorphic map)
.

A meromorphic map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
of complex spaces
X
and
Y
is
a holomorphic map
f^{\circ}:X^{\circ}\to Y

defined on a dense open set
X^{\circ}\subset X
such that

(i)

X\backslash X^{\circ}

is a nowhere-dense analytic subset of
X
;

(ii)

the closure
{\Gamma_{f}}

of the graph
\Gamma_{f^{\circ}}
of
f^{\circ}

in
X\times Y

is an analytic subset of
X\times Y
;

(iii)

the projection
\Gamma_{f}\to X

is a proper mapping.

We say
\Gamma_{f}
the
graph
of the meromorphic map
f
.

Definition 3.57

(Bimeromorphic map)
.

A meromorphic map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
of complex spaces
X
and
Y
is
bimeromorphic
if the projection of
\Gamma_{f}
to
Y
is proper, and off some nowhere-dense analytic subset of
Y
, this projection is biholomorphic.

Lemma 3.58
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a proper surjective holomorphic fibration between irreducible complex normal spaces
X
and
Y
of the same dimension. Then
f
is bimeromorphic.

Démonstration.

Since each fiber of
f
is connected, and
\dim X=\dim Y
, there exists a nowhere-dense analytic subset
A
of
Y
such that
f
is a bijection outside
A
. Since
X
and
Y
are normal, and
f|_{X\backslash f^{-1}(A)}:X\backslash f^{-1}(A)\to Y\backslash A
is a proper holomorphic fibration, it follows that
f|_{X\backslash f^{-1}(A)}
is a biholomorphism. Consider the graph
\Gamma_{f}\subset X\times Y
of
f
. The projection
p:\Gamma_{f}\to Y
is proper as
f
is proper, and
p
a biholomophism over
Y\backslash A
. Therefore,
f
is bimeromorphic.
∎

Theorem 3.59
.

Let
X
be a non-compact smooth quasi-projective variety and
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation.
Then there exists

(a)

a proper bimeromorphic morphism
\sigma:S\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
from a smooth quasi-projective variety
S
;

(b)

a proper birational morphism
\mu:\tilde{X}\to X
from a smooth quasi-projective variety
\tilde{X}
;

(c)

an algebraic morphism
f:\tilde{X}\to S
with general fibers connected ;

such that we have the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{\varrho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

Here
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is the Shafarevich morphism of
\varrho
constructed in
3.49
.

Démonstration.

We apply
[
Kol93
, Corollary 3.5 & Remark 4.1.1]
to conclude that after replacing
X
by its birational model, there are a normal projective variety
Y
and a dominant morphism
f:X\to Y
so that there are at most countably many closed subvarieties
Z_{i}\subsetneqq X
so that for every closed subvariety
W\subset X
such that
W\not\subset\cup Z_{i}
, the image
\varrho\big({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X))]\big)
is finite if and only if
f(W)
is a point.

Claim 3.60
.

For the general fiber
F
of
f:X\to Y
,

\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.

Démonstration.

There exists a non-empty Zariski open
Y^{o}\subset Y
such that the induced
X^{o}\to Y^{o}
is locally trivial fibration of
C^{\infty}
-manifolds.
Hence
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)]
are all the same for fibers
F
over the points of
Y^{o}
.
Now
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for very generic fiber over
Y
, hence the same is true for general fibers.
∎

We may take a finite étale cover
X^{\prime}\to X
such that the induced representation
\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(X^{\prime})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has torsion free image.
Let
\overline{X^{\prime}}
be a partial compactification such that
\varrho^{\prime}
extends to
\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}:\pi_{1}(\overline{X^{\prime}})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
and
\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}
has infinite monodromy at infinity (cf. Proposition
3.19
).
By the proof of
3.49
, we may assume that
X^{\prime}\to X
extends to a finite map
\overline{X^{\prime}}\to\overline{X}
, where
\overline{X}
is a partial compactification of
X
so that the following commutative diagram exists :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{\varrho^{\prime}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\rm sh}_{\varrho}}

(3.16)

Here the horizontal maps are finite and vertical maps are proper.
Since we are assuming that
Y
is projective, we may assume that
f:X\to Y
extends to
\bar{f}:\overline{X}\to Y
.

Claim 3.61
.

There is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

where

is a proper birational morphism from a quasi-projective variety
\widehat{X}
;

\widehat{Y}
,

(c)

\hat{f}
is surjective ;

such that the following property holds :

Let
\bar{f}^{\prime}:\overline{X^{\prime}}\to Y^{\prime}

.
By
3.60
, for the general fiber

By
[
CDY22
, Lemma 2.3]
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{\prime}}

where

(a)

\mu^{\prime}
is a proper birational morphism ;

(b)

\nu^{\prime}
is a birational, not necessarily proper morphism ;

(c)

Y^{\prime\prime}
is smooth,

and a representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(Y^{\prime\prime})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
(f^{\prime\prime})^{*}\tau=(\mu^{\prime})^{*}\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}
.

Claim 3.62
.

f^{\prime\prime}
is a proper morphism with each fiber connected.

Démonstration.

If
f^{\prime\prime}
is not proper, we can find a partial projective compactification
(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime\prime}\supsetneq(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime\prime}
extends to a proper morphism
f^{\prime\prime\prime}:(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime\prime}\to Y
. Then
f^{\prime\prime\prime*}\tau
extends the representation
f^{\prime\prime*}\tau=(\mu^{\prime})^{*}\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}
. It is worth noting that
(\mu^{\prime})^{*}\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}
also has infinite monodromy at infinity by
3.17
. A contradiction is obtained, and thus
f^{\prime\prime}
is a proper morphism.

Since general fibers of
f^{\prime\prime}
is connected and
Y^{\prime\prime}
is smooth, it follows that each fiber of
f
is connected. The claim is proved.
∎

We have the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}

By applying Hironaka-Raynaud-Gruson’s flattening theorem, we can take a birational modification
\tilde{Y}\to Y
such that for the main component
\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}
of
Y^{\prime\prime}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y}
,
\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}\to\tilde{Y}
is flat.
We may assume that
\tilde{Y}
is smooth.
Then we get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y})_{\mathrm{main}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}

Note that
\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}:((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}\to\widetilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}
is proper.
Indeed
(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}\to\widetilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}
is proper, as it is a base change of the proper map
f^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}\hookrightarrow(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}
is a closed immersion,
\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}
is proper.
Also
\varphi
is proper.
Indeed since
(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\to\overline{X}
is proper,
(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}(Y^{\prime\prime}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y})\to\overline{X}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y}
is proper.
Since
((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}\hookrightarrow(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}(Y^{\prime\prime}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y})
is a closed immersion,
((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}\to\overline{X}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y}
is proper.
Hence
\varphi
is proper.
We let
\hat{Y}\subset\tilde{Y}
to be the image, which is a smooth quasi-projective variety as
\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}\to\tilde{Y}
is an open map.
Since
\varphi
is surjective, we have
\tilde{f}((\overline{X}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y})_{\mathrm{main}})\subset\hat{Y}
.
Since
\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}
and
\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}\to\hat{Y}
are surjective,
\tilde{f}:\hat{X}=(\overline{X}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y})_{\mathrm{main}}\to\hat{Y}
is surjective.
Now we set
\hat{X}=(\overline{X}\times_{Y}\tilde{Y})_{\mathrm{main}}
.
Then we have the properties (a)-(c).

Now let
g:Z\to\hat{X}
be a proper morphism from a quasi-projective manifold
Z
such that
\hat{f}\circ g
contracts to a point.
Let
g^{\prime}:Z^{\prime}\to((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}
be a proper map which induces a surjective map
Z^{\prime}\to Z
.
Since
\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime}\to\hat{Y}
is flat,
\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}\circ g^{\prime}
contracts to a point.
Hence
(\tilde{\mu}\circ g^{\prime})^{*}(\mu^{\prime})^{*}\varrho^{\prime}
is trivial, where
\tilde{\mu}:((\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}\times_{Y^{\prime\prime}}\tilde{Y}^{\prime\prime})_{\mathrm{main}}\to(\overline{X^{\prime}})^{\prime}
is the natural map.
Hence
\mathrm{sh}_{\bar{\varrho^{\prime}}}\circ\mu^{\prime}\circ\tilde{\mu}\circ g^{\prime}:Z^{\prime}\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\varrho^{\prime}}(X^{\prime})
contracts to a point.
Hence
\overline{{\rm sh}_{\varrho}}\circ\mu\circ g:Z\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
contracts to a point.
∎

3.61
yields
\sigma:\hat{Y}\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
.
Indeed, we consider
\phi=(\hat{f},\overline{\mathrm{sh}_{\varrho}}\circ\mu):\hat{X}\to\hat{Y}\times\mathrm{Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
.
Then
\phi
is proper since
\overline{\mathrm{sh}_{\varrho}}\circ\mu
is proper.
Hence
\Gamma=\phi(\hat{X})\subset\hat{Y}\times\mathrm{Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is an analytic subset.

Claim 3.63
.

The induced map
t:\Gamma\to\hat{Y}
is an isomorphism of analytic spaces.

Démonstration.

By
3.61
, every fiber of
t:\Gamma\to\hat{Y}
consists of finite points.
Since
\hat{f}:\hat{X}\to\hat{Y}
has general connected fibers,
\Gamma\to\hat{Y}
is injective over a non-empty Zariski open set of
\hat{Y}
.
We prove this.
Let
E\subsetneqq\hat{Y}
be a proper Zariski closed set such that
\Gamma\backslash t^{-1}(E)\to\hat{Y}\backslash E
is injective.
To show that
t:\Gamma\to\hat{Y}
is injective, we assume contrary that there exists
y\in\hat{Y}
such that
t^{-1}(y)=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}\}
consists
k
points with
k\geq 2
.
Let
U\subset\hat{Y}
be a connected open neighbourhood of
y
.
Then by
[
GR84
, p. 166]
,
t^{-1}(U)\backslash t^{-1}(E)\to U\backslash E
is an isomorphism.
Since
U
is normal,
U\backslash E
is connected.
Hence
t^{-1}(U)\backslash t^{-1}(E)
is connected.
In particular,
t^{-1}(U)
is connected.
On the other hand, we may take disjoint open neighbourhoods
V_{1},\ldots,V_{k}
of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.
We may assume that
\partial V_{1},\ldots,\partial V_{k}
are compact and
y\not\in t(\partial V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\partial V_{k})
.
Then we may take a connected open neighbourhood
U
of
y
such that
\overline{U}\cap t(\partial V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\partial V_{k})=\emptyset
.
Then for each
i=1,\ldots,k
,
t^{-1}(U)\cap V_{i}(\ni x_{i})
is non-empty and
\overline{t^{-1}(U)\cap V_{i}}\subset V_{i}
.
Hence
t^{-1}(U)
is not connected.
This is a contradiction.
Hence
t:\Gamma\to\hat{Y}
is injective. Note that
t
is surjective, for
\hat{f}
is surjective (cf.
3.61
).
Hence
t
is an isomorphism by
[
GR84
, p. 166]
.
∎

Hence we obtain the analytic map
\sigma:\hat{Y}\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
whose graph is
\Gamma
.

Since
\mu
and
\overline{{\rm sh}_{\varrho}}
are proper, and
\hat{f}
is surjective,
\sigma
is proper. Moreover,
\sigma
has connected fibers since
\overline{\rm sh_{\varrho}}\circ\mu
is a proper holomorphic fibration.
We claim that
\sigma:\hat{Y}\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is bimeromorphic.
Indeed, since
\sigma:\hat{Y}\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is a proper holomorphic fibration, it suffices to prove that
\dim\hat{Y}=\dim{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
by
3.58
.
Assume, for the sake of contradiction, we have
\dim\hat{Y}>\dim{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
.
Since
\hat{Y}
is quasi-projective and
\sigma
is proper, each fiber of
\sigma
is thus algebraic subset of
\hat{Y}
.
Let
F
be a generic fiber of
\sigma
.
Then
\nu(F)
is not a point.
Let
Z\subset X
satisfies that the Zariski closure of
f(Z)
is equal to that of
\nu(F)
.
Then
\mathrm{sh}_{\varrho}(Z)
is a point.
Therefore,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite.
On the other hand, since
Z
is not contracted by
f
,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is infinite.
This is a contradiction.
Hence
\dim\hat{Y}=\dim{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
, therefore
\sigma:\hat{Y}\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is bimeromorphic.
∎

It is worth noting that while
\sigma:S\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
is a proper bimeromorphic map, showing that
{\rm Sh}\varrho(X)
is quasi-projective requires understanding the structure of the Shafarevich morphism, as illustrated in the following example communicated with us by Kollár.

Remark 3.64
.

Let
g:X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
be a family of K3 surfaces over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, such that
the generic fiber of
g
has Picard number 1, but infinitely many fibers
X_{t_{i}}:=g^{-1}(t_{i})
contain a (-2)-curve
C_{i}\subset X_{t_{i}}
with
\{t_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}>0}
a discrete set escaping to infinity.
One can contract all the
C_{i}
to get a normal 3-fold
Y
with infinitely many singular points. Then
f:X\to Y
is a proper bimeromorphic map, but not a birational morphism.

Remark 3.65
.

In the proof of
3.59
, we apply the existence of
Shafarevich maps
established by Kollár
[
Kol93
]
.

Recall that when
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
underlies a
\mathbb{Z}
-VHS and has infinite monodromy at infinity, the Shafarevich morphism
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
arises as the Stein factorization of the period mapping
X\to\mathscr{D}/\Gamma
. Here,
\mathscr{D}
denotes the period domain of the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}
-VHS, and
\Gamma
represents the monodromy group of
\varrho
. In this context, Sommese
[
Som78
]
proved
3.59
by utilizing Hörmander-Andreotti-Vesentini
L^{2}
-method. As a result,
3.59
represents a significant extension of the results in
[
Som78
]
, while also offering a simpler proof, even when
\varrho
underlies a
\mathbb{Z}
-VHS. Notably, proving
3.55
using the
L^{2}
-method poses a highly challenging problem.

3.7.
Construction of Shafarevich morphism (III)

In this subsection we will prove
B
.
Recall that in Step 1 of the proof of
3.49
we proved the following result.

Lemma 3.66
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety, and let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation.
Let
X^{\prime}
be a smooth partial compactification of
X
such that
X^{\prime}
is quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity and
X^{\prime}-X
is a simple normal crossing divisor.
Then we may construct
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
such that
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}
extends to a proper map
X^{\prime}\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
. ∎

Lemma 3.67
.

Assume
X
is smooth.
Given a sequence of representations
\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})\}
such that
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i+1})\subset\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i})
.
Then, there exist
i_{0}
and a smooth partial compactification
X^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}
is quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity for every
\varrho_{i}
with
i\geq i_{0}
.
Moreover
X^{\prime}-X
is a simple normal crossing divisor.

Proof of
3.67
.

The proof is a modification of the proof of
proposition

3.19
.
Let
\overline{X}
be a compactification such that the boundary divisor
\overline{X}-X
is simple normal crossing.
We shall show that after a sequence
\overline{X}^{(n)}\to\overline{X}^{(n-1)}\to\cdots\to\overline{X}
of admissible blow-ups,
\overline{X}^{(n)}
becomes a compactification with the good property.

We consider all sets
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
of boundary divisors such that
\bigcap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
and the monodromy matrices
A_{\varrho_{i}}
for these
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
and construct
\Lambda_{\varrho_{i}}=\{S_{\lambda}\}
.
This
\Lambda_{\varrho_{i}}
is maximum.

For each
\varrho_{i}
, we proceed the proof of
proposition

3.19
.
Namely by
3.21
and
3.22
, there exists a sequence
\overline{X}^{(n_{i})}\to\cdots\to\overline{X}
of admissible blow-ups such that
\mu(S_{\lambda}^{(n_{i})})=0
for all
S_{\lambda}\in\Lambda_{\varrho_{i}}
.
Note that
\Lambda^{(n_{i})}=\{S_{\lambda}^{(n_{i})}\}
is maximum (cf.
3.23
).

Let
f:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to X
such that
f(0)\in\overline{X}^{(n_{i})}\backslash X
.
If
f^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
has infinite image, then
f^{*}\varrho_{i^{\prime}}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i^{\prime}}}(\mathbb{C})
has infinite image for all
i^{\prime}\geq i
.
This follows from the assumption
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i^{\prime}})\subset\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i})
.
So we consider the case that
f^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image.

Claim 3.68
.

There exists
i_{0}
such that if
f(0)\in\overline{X}^{(n_{i_{0}})}\backslash X
is contained in only one irreducible boundary component and
f^{*}\varrho_{i_{0}}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i_{0}}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image, then
f^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image for all
i\geq i_{0}
.

Démonstration.

Let
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
be boundary divisors on
\partial{X}
such that
\bigcap D_{j}\not=\emptyset
as above.
We associate a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
V=\mathbb{Q}^{l}
generated by the formal basis
(D_{1}),\ldots,(D_{l})
.
Let
f:\mathbb{D}^{*}\to X
such that
f(0)\in\bigcap D_{j}
, we associate
(f)\in V
by
(f)=\sum b_{j}(D_{j})
, where
b_{j}=\mathrm{ord}_{0}f^{*}D_{j}
.
We set
\Sigma=\{(f)\in V\}
and

\Sigma_{i}=\{(f)\in V;\text{$f^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})$ has finite image}\}.

Let
W_{i}\subset V
be the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space spanned by
\Sigma_{i}
.
Then we have

\Sigma_{i}=\Sigma\cap W_{i}.

(3.17)

Indeed
\Sigma_{i}\subset\Sigma\cap W_{i}
is obvious.
To show the converse, we take
(f)\in\Sigma\cap W_{i}
.
By
(f)\in W_{i}
, there exist
(f_{1}),\ldots,(f_{k})\in\Sigma_{i}
such that
(f)=c_{1}(f_{1})+\cdots+c_{k}(f_{k})
.
Then using the monodromy matrix
A_{\varrho_{i}}
, we have
A_{\varrho_{i}}(f)=c_{1}A_{\varrho_{i}}(f_{1})+\cdots+c_{k}A_{\varrho_{i}}(f_{k})=0
.
Hence
(f)\in\Sigma_{i}
.
This shows (
).

By the definition of
\Sigma_{i}
, we have
\Sigma\supset\Sigma_{1}\supset\Sigma_{2}\supset\cdots
.
Hence
W_{1}\supset W_{2}\supset\cdots
.
Thus there exists
i
such that
W_{i}=W_{i+1}=\cdots
.
Hence by (
), we have
\Sigma_{i}=\Sigma_{i+1}=\cdots
.
In particular, if
f^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image, then
f^{*}\varrho_{i^{\prime}}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i^{\prime}}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image for all
i^{\prime}\geq i
.

So far, we have fixed
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
and chose
i=i_{\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}}
.
Next we consider all such
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}
and chose
i_{0}
big enough such that
i_{0}
is greater than every
i_{\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}\}}
above.

Now suppose
f(0)\in\overline{X}^{(n_{i_{0}})}\backslash X
is contained in only one irreducible boundary component and
f^{*}\varrho_{i_{0}}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i_{0}}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image.
Let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{l}
be boundary divisors in
\overline{X}
so that
f(0)\in D\subset\overline{X}
.
Then by the choice of
i_{0}
,
f^{*}\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{D}^{*})\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image for all
i\geq i_{0}
.
The proof of
3.68
is completed.
∎

Now we return to the proof of
3.67
.
We remove all boundary divisors
D
from
\overline{X}^{(n_{i_{0}})}
such that local monodromy with respect to
\varrho_{i_{0}}
at
D
is infinite to get a desired partial compactification
X^{\prime}
of
X
.
∎

Lemma 3.69
.

Let
A_{N}
be the set of all
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho)\subset\pi_{1}(X)
for the reductive representations
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then
A_{N}
is countable.
In particular,
\cup_{N}A_{N}
is countable.

Démonstration.

We use the notation in the proof of
3.25
.
Given a reductive representation
\varrho
, we define
Z_{\varrho}\subset M
by
Z_{\varrho}=\cap_{\gamma\in\mathrm{ker}(\varrho)}W_{\gamma}
.
Then
Z_{\varrho}\subset M
is a Zariski closed set.
Using
Z_{\varrho}
, we may construct
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho)\subset\pi_{1}(X)
by
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho)=\{\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X);Z_{\varrho}\subset W_{\gamma}\}
.
By the Noetherian property, there exists
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{l}
such that
Z_{\varrho}=W_{\gamma_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap W_{\gamma_{l}}
.
In this way, we may label
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho)
a finite subset
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{l}\}\subset\pi_{1}(X)
.
Since
\pi_{1}(X)
is countable, it has only countable finite subsets.
Hence
A_{N}
is countable.
∎

Lemma 3.70
.

Let
V
be a quasi-projective normal variety and let
(f_{\lambda}:V\to S_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a family of proper morphisms into normal complex spaces
S_{\lambda}
.
Then there exist a normal complex space
S_{\infty}
and a proper morphism
f_{\infty}:V\to S_{\infty}
such that

(a)

for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, there exists a morphism
e_{\lambda}:S_{\infty}\to S_{\lambda}
such that
f_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda}\circ f_{\infty}
;

(b)

for every algebraic subvariety
Z\subset V
, if
f_{\lambda}(Z)
is a point for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
, then
f_{\infty}(Z)
is a point.

Démonstration.

The proof is a modification of that of
1.28
.
Let
E_{\lambda}\subset V\times V
be defined by

E_{\lambda}=\{(x,x^{\prime})\in V\times V;f_{\lambda}(x)=f_{\lambda}(x^{\prime})\}.

Then
E_{\lambda}\subset V\times V
is an analytic subset.
Indeed,
E_{\lambda}=(f_{\lambda},f_{\lambda})^{-1}(\Delta_{\lambda})
, where
(f_{\lambda},f_{\lambda}):V\times V\to S_{\lambda}\times S_{\lambda}
is the morphism defined by
(f_{\lambda},f_{\lambda})(x,x^{\prime})=(f_{\lambda}(x),f_{\lambda}(x^{\prime}))
and
\Delta_{\lambda}\subset S_{\lambda}\times S_{\lambda}
is the diagonal.

Let
K_{1}\subset K_{2}\subset\cdots\subset V
be a sequence of domains such that

—

\overline{K_{n}}
is compact and
\overline{K_{n}}\subset K_{n+1}
for all
n
, and

—

V=\cup_{n}K_{n}
.

Given
n
, we take a finite subset
\Lambda_{n}\subset\Lambda
as follows.
Note that
\cap_{\lambda\in\Lambda}E_{\lambda}\cap(K_{n}\times K_{n})
is an analytic subset of
K_{n}\times K_{n}
.
Since
\overline{K_{n}\times K_{n}}
is compact, we may take a finite subset
\Lambda_{n}\subset\Lambda
such that

\cap_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}E_{\lambda}\cap(K_{n}\times K_{n})=\cap_{\lambda\in\Lambda}E_{\lambda}\cap(K_{n}\times K_{n}).

(3.18)

We may assume that
\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\Lambda
.
Let
g_{n}:V\to\Sigma_{n}
be the Stein factorization of the proper map
(f_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}:V\to\Pi_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}S_{\lambda}
.
Then
\Sigma_{n}
is normal and
g_{n}:V\to\Sigma_{n}
is proper, hence surjective.
By (
), for a closed subvariety
Z\subset V
such that
Z\subset K_{n}
, we have

g_{n}(Z)
is a point

\Longleftrightarrow

f_{\lambda}(Z)
is a point for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

(3.19)

By
\Lambda_{n}\subset\Lambda_{n+1}
, we have a natural morphism
\Sigma_{n+1}\to\Sigma_{n}
.
We set
\varphi_{n}:\Sigma_{n}\to\Sigma_{1}
for the induced map.

Let
H_{1}\subset H_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\Sigma_{1}
be a sequence of domains such that

—

\overline{H_{k}}
is compact and
\overline{H_{k}}\subset H_{k+1}
for all
k
, and

—

\Sigma_{1}=\cup_{k}H_{k}
.

We take this sequence inductively as follows.
Set
H_{0}=\emptyset
.
Assume we are given
H_{0}\subset\cdots\subset H_{k}
, where
k\geq 0
.
Since
g_{1}(\overline{K_{k+1}})\cup\overline{H_{k}}
is compact, we may take a domain
H_{k+1}
so that
g_{1}(\overline{K_{k+1}})\cup\overline{H_{k}}\subset H_{k+1}
and
\overline{H_{k+1}}
is compact.
Now since
g_{1}:V\to\Sigma_{1}
is surjective, we have
\Sigma_{1}=\cup_{k}g_{1}(K_{k})\subset\cup_{k}H_{k}
.

For all
k
, we claim that there exists
n_{k}
such that the induced maps
\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k})\leftarrow\varphi_{n_{k}+1}^{-1}(H_{k})\leftarrow\cdots
are all isomorphisms.
Indeed since
g_{1}:V\to\Sigma_{1}
is proper, we may take
n_{k}
so that
g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})\subset K_{n_{k}}
.
Let
n\geq n_{k}
.
Since every fiber of
g_{n}:V\to\Sigma_{n}
is connected, (
) yields that every fiber of
g_{n}:V\to\Sigma_{n}
over
\varphi_{n}^{-1}(H_{k})\subset\Sigma_{n}
is contracted to a point by
g_{n+1}:V\to\Sigma_{n+1}
.
Hence
\varphi_{n+1}^{-1}(H_{k})\to\varphi_{n}^{-1}(H_{k})
is injective.
Since this is also surjective, the induced maps
\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k})\leftarrow\varphi_{n_{k}+1}^{-1}(H_{k})\leftarrow\cdots
are all isomorphism, for
\Sigma_{n}
are normal (cf.
[
GR84
, p. 166]
).
Hence
\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k})=\varphi_{n_{k+1}}^{-1}(H_{k})\subset\varphi_{n_{k+1}}^{-1}(H_{k+1})
.
We set

S_{\infty}=\cup\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k}).

The map
f_{\infty}:V\to S_{\infty}
is defined by
f_{\infty}|_{g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})}=g_{n_{k}}|_{g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})}
.
We note that
g_{n_{k}}|_{g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})}:g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})\to\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k})
is proper.
Hence
f_{\infty}:V\to S_{\infty}
is proper.

Now we prove the property (a).
We take
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Let
Z\subset V
be an algebraic subvariety such that
f_{\infty}(Z)
is a point in
S_{\infty}
.
We shall show that
f_{\lambda}(Z)
is a point.
Indeed we take
k
such that
f_{\infty}(Z)\in\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k})
.
Then
Z\subset g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})\subset K_{n_{k}}
and
g_{n_{k}}(Z)
is a point.
Hence by (
),
f_{\lambda}(Z)
is a point.
This shows that
f_{\lambda}:V\to S_{\lambda}
factors through
f_{\infty}:V\to S_{\infty}
.
Thus we get
e_{\lambda}:S_{\infty}\to S_{\lambda}
.

To show the property (b), we take an algebraic subvariety
Z\subset V
such that
f_{\lambda}(Z)
is a point for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Then
g_{n}(Z)
is a point of
\Sigma_{n}
for every
n
.
In particular,
g_{1}(Z)
is a point in
\Sigma_{1}
.
We take
k
such that
g_{1}(Z)\in H_{k}
.
Then
Z\subset g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})
.
Since
g_{n_{k}}
and
f_{\infty}
coincide as morphisms
g_{1}^{-1}(H_{k})\to\varphi_{n_{k}}^{-1}(H_{k})
, where
\varphi_{n_{k}}(H_{k})
is contained in both
\Sigma_{n_{k}}
and
S_{\infty}
.
Hence
f_{\infty}(Z)
is a point of
S_{\infty}
.
∎

Lemma 3.71
.

Assume
X
is smooth.
Let
\Sigma=\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}
be a sequence of reductive representations such that
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i})\subset\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i-1})
.
Then there exists a dominant holomorphic map with general fibers connected
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\Sigma}(X)
onto a complex normal space
{\rm Sh}_{\Sigma}(X)
such that for closed subvariety
Z\subset X
,
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma}(Z)
is a point if and only if
\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for every
i
.

Démonstration.

By
3.67
, there exist
i_{0}
and a smooth partial compactification
X^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}
is quasi-infinite monodromy at infinity for every
\varrho_{i}
with
i\geq i_{0}
. By
3.66
, there exists a proper surjective holomorphic map
f_{i}:X^{\prime}\to S_{i}
for each
i\geq i_{0}
such that the Shafarevich morphism
{\rm sh}_{\varrho_{i}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho_{i}}(X)
of
\varrho_{i}
is the restriction
f_{i}|_{X}
.
By
3.70
, there exists a surjective proper holomorphic fibration
f_{\infty}:X^{\prime}\to S_{\infty}
onto a complex normal space
S_{\infty}
and holomorphic maps
e_{i}:S_{\infty}\to S_{i}
such that

(a)

f_{i}=e_{i}\circ f_{\infty}
;

(b)

for every algebraic subvariety
Z\subset X^{\prime}
, if
f_{i}(Z)
is a point for every
i\geq i_{0}
, then
f_{\infty}(Z)
is a point.

For an algebraic subvariety
Z
of
X
with its closure denoted as
\overline{Z}
in
X^{\prime}
,
f_{\infty}(\overline{Z})
is a point if and only if
f_{i}(\overline{Z})
is a point for any
i\geq i_{0}
. Additionally, by the property of the Shafarevich morphism the same holds true if and only if

\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for every for any
i\geq i_{0}
, and, given that
\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i})\subset\mathrm{ker}(\varrho_{i-1})
, it is equivalent to
\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
for any
i\geq 1
. To conclude the proof, let
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\Sigma}(X)
be the restriction of
f_{\infty}
to
X
.
∎

Corollary 3.72
.

Let
X
be a quasi-projective normal variety.
Let
\Sigma
be a (non-empty) set of reductive representations
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N_{\varrho}}(\mathbb{C})
. Then there is a dominant holomorphic map
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\Sigma}(X)
with general fibers connected onto a complex normal space such that for closed subvariety
Z\subset X
,
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma}(Z)
is a point if and only if
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for every
\varrho\in\Sigma
.

Démonstration.

By Step 2 of the proof of
3.49
, we may assume that
X
is smooth. By
3.69
, we can take a set of reductive representations
\Sigma_{1}:=\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C})\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}
such that
\varrho_{i}\in\Sigma
, and for any
\varrho\in\Sigma
, there exists
\varrho_{i}
such that
\ker\varrho_{i}=\ker\varrho
. Therefore, if
\Gamma\subset\pi_{1}(X)
is a subgroup such that
\varrho_{i}(\Gamma)
is finite for each
\varrho_{i}\in\Sigma_{1}
, then
\varrho(\Gamma)
is finite for each
\varrho\in\Sigma
.

Define
\tau_{i}
to be the product representation

(\varrho_{1},\ldots,\varrho_{i}):\pi_{1}(X)\to\prod_{j=1}^{i}{\rm GL}_{N_{j}}(\mathbb{C}).

Then it is reductive and we have
\ker\tau_{i}\subset\ker\tau_{i+1}
. Let
\Sigma_{2}:=\{\tau_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}
. By
3.71
, there exists a dominant holomorphic map
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma_{2}}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\Sigma_{2}}(X)
with general fibers connected onto a complex normal space such that for closed subvariety
Z\subset X
,
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma_{2}}(Z)
is a point if and only if
\tau_{i}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for every
\tau_{i}\in\Sigma_{2}
.

We note that
\tau_{i}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite if and only if
\varrho_{j}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
are all finite for each
j=1,\ldots,i
. Therefore,
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma_{2}}(Z)
is a point if and only if
\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for every
\varrho_{i}\in\Sigma_{1}
, and if only only if
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is finite for every
\varrho\in\Sigma
. Let
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma}
be
{\rm sh}_{\Sigma_{2}}
, and thus, the theorem is proven.
∎

4.
Proof of the reductive Shafarevich conjecture

The goal of this section is to provide proofs for
D
and
C
when
X
is a
smooth
projective variety. It is important to note that our methods differs from the approach presented in
[
Eys04
]
, although we do follow the general strategy in that work.

In this section, we will use the notation
\mathcal{D}G
to denote the derived group of any given group
G
. Throughout the section, our focus is on non-archimedean local fields with characteristic zero. More precisely, we consider finite extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
for some prime
p
.

4.1.
Reduction map of representation into algebraic tori

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. Let
a:X\to A
be the Albanese morphism of
X
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
P\subset A
be an abelian subvariety of the Albanese variety
A
of
X
and
K
be a non-archimedean local field. If
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K)
factors through
\sigma:\pi_{1}(A/P)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K)
, then the Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction map
s_{\tau}:X\to S_{\tau}
factors through the Stein factorization of the map
q:X\to A/P
.

Démonstration.

As
\tau=q^{*}\sigma
, it follows that for each connected component
F
of the fiber of
q:X\to A/P
,
\tau(\pi_{1}(F))=\{1\}
. Therefore,
F
is contracted by
s_{\tau}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
P\subset A
be an abelian subvariety of
A
. Let
N
be a Zariski dense open set of the image
j:M_{\rm B}(A/P,1)\to M_{\rm B}(A,1)
where we consider
M_{\rm B}(A/P,1)
and
M_{\rm B}(A,1)
as algebraic tori defined over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then there are non-archimedean local fields
K_{i}
and a family of representations
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
such that

—

\tau_{i}\in N(K_{i})
, where we use the natural identification
M^{0}_{\rm B}(X,1)\simeq M_{\rm B}(A,1)
. Here
M^{0}_{\rm B}(X,1)
denotes the connected component of
M^{0}_{\rm B}(X,1)
containing the trivial representation.

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{\bm{\tau}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
.

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
defined over
S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Démonstration.

Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}
be a basis of
\pi_{1}(A/P)\simeq H_{1}(A/P,\mathbb{Z})
. Note that
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-scheme
M_{\rm B}(A/P,1)\simeq(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times})^{m}
. Denote by
S\subset U(1)\cap\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
the set of roots of unity. Then
S
is Zariski dense in
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
. Since
j^{-1}(N)
is a Zariski dense open set of
M_{\rm B}(A/P,1)
, it follows that there are
\{a_{ij}\}_{i,j=1,\ldots,m}\in\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
and representations
\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(A/P)\to\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}

defined by
\varrho_{i}(e_{j})=a_{ij}

such that

—

[\varrho_{i}]\in j^{-1}(N)(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
;

—

If
i=j
,
a_{ij}\in\bar{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}\setminus U(1)
;

—

If
i\neq j
,
a_{ij}\in S
.

Consider a number field
k_{i}
containing
a_{i1},\ldots,a_{im}
endowed with a discrete non-archimedean valuation
v_{i}:k_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
v_{i}(a_{ii})\neq 0
. Then
v_{i}(a_{ij})=0
for every
j\neq i
. Indeed, for every
j\neq i
, since
a_{ij}
is a root of unity, there exists
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
a_{ij}^{\ell}=1
. It follows that
0=v(a_{ij}^{\ell})=\ell v(a_{ij})
. Let
K_{i}
be the non-archimedean local field which is the completion of
k_{i}
with respect to
v_{i}
. It follows that each
\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(A/P)\to K_{i}^{\times}
is unbounded. Consider
\nu_{i}:\pi_{1}(A/P)\to\mathbb{R}
by composing
\varrho_{i}
with
v_{i}:K_{i}^{\times}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then
\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m}\}\subset H^{1}(A/P,\mathbb{R})
is a basis for the
\mathbb{R}
-linear space
H^{1}(A/P,\mathbb{R})
. It follows that
\nu_{i}(e_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for any
i,j
. Let
\eta_{i}\in H^{0}(A/P,\Omega_{A/P}^{1})
be the
(1,0)
-part of the Hodge decomposition of
\nu_{i}
. Therefore,
\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}\}
spans the
\mathbb{C}
-linear space
H^{0}(A/P,\Omega_{A/P}^{1})
. Hence
\sum_{i=1}^{m}i\eta_{i}\wedge\overline{\eta_{i}}
is a Kähler form on
A/P
. Let
\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to K_{i}^{\times}
be the composition of
\varrho_{i}
with
\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(A/P)
.

Let
q:A\to A/P
be the quotient map. Let
P^{\prime}
the largest abelian subvariety of
A
such that
q^{*}\eta_{i}|_{P^{\prime}}\equiv 0
for each
i
. Since
\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}\}
spans
H^{0}(B,\Omega_{B}^{1})
, it follows that
P^{\prime}=P
. Therefore, the reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{\bm{\tau}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
with
g:S_{\bm{\tau}}\to A/P
be the finite morphism. According to
1.24
,
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}=g^{*}\sum_{i=1}^{m}i\eta_{i}\wedge\overline{\eta_{i}}
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{m}i\eta_{i}\wedge\overline{\eta_{i}}
is a Kähler form on
A/P
, it follows that
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class by
1.13
.
The lemma is proved.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. If
\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}({X},1)
is an absolutely constructible subset. Consider the reduction map
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
defined in
3.1
. Then there is a family of representations
{\bm{\varrho}}:=\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}
where
K_{i}
are non-archimedean local fields such that

—

For each
i=1,\ldots,\ell
,
\varrho_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}(K_{i})
;

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
of
{\bm{\varrho}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
defined over
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Démonstration.

Let
A
be the Albanese variety of
X
. Since
\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}({X},1)
is an absolute constructible subset, by
1.19
and
1.16
, there are abelian subvarieties
\{P_{i}\subset A\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
and torsion points
v_{i}\in M_{\rm B}(X,1)(\bar{\mathbb{Q}})
such that
\mathfrak{C}=\cup_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}.N_{i}^{\circ}
. Here
N_{i}
is the image in
M^{0}_{\rm B}(X,1)\simeq M_{\rm B}(A,1)
of the natural morphism
M_{\rm B}(A/P_{i},1)\to M_{\rm B}(A,1)
and
N_{i}^{\circ}
is a Zariski dense open subset of
N_{i}
such that
N_{i}\backslash N_{i}^{\circ}
is defined over
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
k
be a number field such that
v_{i}\in M_{\rm B}(X,1)(k)
for each
i
.

Claim 4.4
.

Denote by
P:=\cap_{i=1}^{m}P_{i}
. Then
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
.

Démonstration.

Let
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K)
be a reductive representation with
K
a non-archimedean local field such that
\tau\in\mathfrak{C}(K)
. Note that the reduction map
s_{\tau}
is the same if we replace
K
by a finite extension. We thus can assume that
k\subset K
. Note that there exists some
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
such that
[v_{i}^{-1}.\tau]\in N_{i}(K)
. Write
\varrho:=v_{i}^{-1}.\tau
. Since
v_{i}
is a torsion element, it follows that
v_{i}(\pi_{1}(X))
is finite, and thus the reduction map
s_{\varrho}
coincides with
s_{\tau}
. Since
\varrho
factors through
\pi_{1}(A/P_{i})\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K)
, by
4.1

s_{\varrho}
factors through the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P_{i}
. Hence
s_{\varrho}
factors through the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
. By
3.1
, it follows that
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
factors through the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
.

Fix any
i
. By
4.2
there are non-archimedean local fields
K_{j}
and a family of reductive representations
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{j}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K_{j})\}_{j=1,\ldots,n}
such that

—

\tau_{j}\in N_{i}^{\circ}(K_{j})
.

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{\bm{\tau}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P_{i}
.

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
over
S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

We can replace
K_{i}
by a finite extension such that
k\subset K_{i}
for each
K_{i}
. Then
v_{i}.\tau_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}(K_{i})
for every
i
. Note that the Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction map
s_{v_{i}.\tau_{j}}:X\to S_{v_{i}.\tau_{j}}
coincides with
s_{\tau_{j}}:X\to S_{\tau_{j}}
. Therefore, the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P_{i}
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Since this holds for each
i
, it follows that the Stein factorization
X\to A/P_{1}\times\cdots\times A/P_{m}
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Note that the Stein factorization
X\to A/P_{1}\times\cdots\times A/P_{m}
coincides with the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
. Therefore, the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. The claim is proved.
∎

By the above arguments, for each
i
, there exists a family of reductive representations into non-archimedean local fields
{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}:=\{\varrho_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K_{ij})\}_{j=1,\ldots,k_{i}}
such that

—

\varrho_{ij}\in\mathfrak{C}(K_{ij})

—

s_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P_{i}

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
defined over
S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}\}
is a Kähler class.

By the above claim, we know that
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{m}}
. Then for the representation
{\bm{\varrho}}:=\{\varrho_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K_{ij})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m;j=1,\ldots,k_{i}}
,
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to A/P
hence
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Moreover, the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}^{*}T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
where
g_{i}:S_{\mathfrak{C}}\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
is the natural map. As
S_{\mathfrak{C}}\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{m}}
is finite, by
1.13

\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}\}
is Kähler.
∎

Let us prove the main result in this subsection.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and let
T
be an algebraic tori defined over some number field
k
. Let
\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}({X},T)(\mathbb{C})
be an absolutely constructible subset. Consider the reduction map
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
. Then there is a family of reductive representations
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to T(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
where
K_{i}
are non-archimedean local fields containing
k
such that

—

For each
i=1,\ldots,N
,
[\tau_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}(K_{i})
;

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
of
{\bm{\tau}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
over
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
defined in
1.24
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Démonstration.

We replace
k
by a finite extension such that
T
is split over
k
. Then we have
T\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m,k}^{\ell}
. Note that this does not change the reduction map
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
. We take
p_{i}:T\to\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
to be the
i
-th projection which is a
k
-morphism. It induces a morphism of
k
-schemes
\psi_{i}:M_{\rm B}(X,T)\to M_{\rm B}(X,{\rm GL}_{1})
. By
1.20
,
\mathfrak{C}_{i}:=\psi_{i}(\mathfrak{C})
is also an absolutely constructible subset. Consider the reduction maps
\{s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}
defined by
3.1
.

Claim 4.6
.

s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the Stein factorization of
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times s_{\mathfrak{C}_{\ell}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\mathfrak{C}_{\ell}}
.

Démonstration.

Let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to T(K)
be any reductive representation where
K
is a non-archimedean local field containing
k
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(K)
. Write
\varrho_{i}=p_{i}\circ\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K)
. Then
[\varrho_{i}]=\psi_{i}([\varrho])\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(K)
. Note that for any subgroup
\Gamma\subset\pi_{1}(X)
,
\varrho(\Gamma)
is bounded if and only if
\varrho_{i}(\Gamma)
is bounded for any
i
. Therefore,
s_{\varrho}:X\to S_{\varrho}
is the Stein factorization of
X\to S_{\varrho_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\varrho_{\ell}}
. Hence
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
factors through the Stein factorization of
X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\mathfrak{C}_{\ell}}
.

On the other hand, consider any
\varrho_{i}\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(K)
where
K
is a non-archimedean local field containing
k
. Then there is a finite extension
L
of
K
such that

—

there is a reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to T(L)
with
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(L)
;

—

p_{i}\circ\varrho=\varrho_{i}
.

By the above argument,
s_{\varrho_{i}}:X\to S_{\varrho_{i}}
factors through
s_{\varrho}:X\to S_{\varrho}
. Note that
s_{\varrho}
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. It follows that the Stein factorization of
X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\mathfrak{C}_{\ell}}
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
. The claim is proved.
∎

We now apply
4.3
to conclude that for each
i
, there exists a family of reductive representations into non-archimedean local fields
{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}:=\{\varrho_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{1}(K_{ij})\}_{j=1,\ldots,k_{i}}
such that

—

\varrho_{ij}\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(K_{ij})
;

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
of
{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
;

—

for the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
defined over
S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Denote by
{\bm{\varrho}}:=\{\varrho_{ij}\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell;j=1,\ldots,k_{i}}
. Then
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
by the above claim. Then
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
is a Kähler class.

By the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
, we can find a finite extension
L_{ij}
of
K_{ij}
such that

—

there is a reductive representation
\tau_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to T(L_{ij})
with
[\tau_{ij}]\in\mathfrak{C}(L_{ij})
;

—

p_{i}\circ\tau_{ij}=\varrho_{ij}
.

Therefore, for the family
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{ij}\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell;j=1,\ldots,k_{i}}
,
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
by the above claim.
Note that for any
