i,j
, there exists an morphism
e_{ij}:S_{\tau_{ij}}\to S_{\varrho_{ij}}
such that
s_{\varrho_{ij}}:X\to S_{\varrho_{ij}}
factors through
e_{ij}
. We also note that
e_{ij}^{*}T_{\varrho_{ij}}\leq T_{\tau_{ij}}
for the canonical currents. It follows that
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}\leq T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
(note that
S_{\bm{\tau}}=S_{\bm{\varrho}}=S_{\mathfrak{C}}
). Therefore,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class. We prove the theorem.
∎

4.2.
Some criterion for representation into tori

We recall a lemma in
[
CDY22
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
G
be an almost simple algebraic group over the non-archimedean local field
K
. Let
\Gamma\subset G(K)
be a finitely generated subgroup so that

—

it is a Zariski dense subgroup in
G
,

—

it is not contained in any bounded subgroup of
G(K)
.

Let
\Upsilon
be a normal subgroup of
\Gamma
which is
bounded
. Then
\Upsilon
must be finite.

This lemma enables us to prove the following result.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
G
be a reductive algebraic group over the non-archimedean local field
K
of characteristic zero. Let
X
be a projective manifold and let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to G(K)
be a Zariski dense representation. If
\varrho(\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(X))
is bounded, then after replacing
K
by some finite extension, for the reductive representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to G/\mathcal{D}G(K)
which is the composition of
\varrho
with
G\to G/\mathcal{D}G
, the reduction map
s_{\tau}:X\to S_{\tau}
coincides with
s_{\varrho}:X\to S_{\varrho}
.

Démonstration.

Since
G
is reductive, then after replacing
K
by a finite extension, there is an isogeny
G\to H_{1}\times\cdots\times H_{k}\times T
, where
H_{i}
are almost simple algebraic groups over
K
and
T=G/\mathcal{D}G
is an algebraic tori over
K
.Write
G^{\prime}:=H_{1}\times\cdots\times H_{k}\times T
. We denote by
\varrho^{\prime}:\pi_{1}(X)\to G^{\prime}(K)
the induced representation by the above isogeny.

Claim 4.9
.

The Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction map
s_{\varrho}:X\to S_{\varrho}
coincides with
s_{\varrho^{\prime}}:X\to S_{\varrho^{\prime}}
.

Démonstration.

It suffices to prove that, for any subgroup
\Gamma
of
\pi_{1}(X)
,
\varrho(\Gamma)
is bounded if and only if
\varrho^{\prime}(\Gamma)
is bounded. Note that we have the following short exact sequence of algebraic groups

0\to\mu\to G\to G^{\prime}\to 0

where
\mu
is finite. Then
we have

0\to\mu(K)\to G(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}G^{\prime}(K)\to H^{1}(K,\mu),

where
H^{1}(K,\mu)
is the Galois cohomology. Note that
\mu(K)
is finite. Since
K
is a finite extension of some
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, it follows that
H^{1}(K,\mu)
is also finite. Therefore,
f:G(K)\to G^{\prime}(K)
has finite kernel and cokernel. Therefore,
\varrho(\Gamma)
is bounded if and only if
\varrho^{\prime}(\Gamma)
is bounded.
∎

Set
\Gamma:=\varrho^{\prime}(\pi_{1}(X))
and
\Upsilon:=\varrho^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(X))
. Let
\Upsilon_{i}\subset H_{i}(K)
and
\Gamma_{i}
be the image of
\Upsilon
and
\Gamma
under the projection
G(K)\to H_{i}(K)
. Then
\Gamma_{i}
is Zariski dense in
H_{i}
and
\Upsilon_{i}\triangleleft\Gamma_{i}
is also bounded. Furthermore,
\mathcal{D}\Gamma_{i}=\Upsilon_{i}
.

Claim 4.10
.

\Gamma_{i}
is bounded for every
i
.

Démonstration.

Assuming a contradiction, let’s suppose that some
\Gamma_{i}
is unbounded. Since
\Upsilon_{i}\triangleleft\Gamma_{i}
and
\Upsilon_{i}
is bounded, we can refer to
4.7
which states that
\Upsilon_{i}
must be finite. We may replace
X
with a finite étale cover, allowing us to assume that
\Upsilon_{i}
is trivial. Consequently,
\Gamma_{i}
becomes abelian, which contradicts the fact that
\Gamma_{i}
is Zariski dense in the almost simple algebraic group
H_{i}
.
∎

Based on the previous claim, it follows that the induced representations
\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to H_{i}(K)
are all bounded for every
i
. Consequently, they do not contribute to the reduction map of
s_{\varrho^{\prime}}:X\to S_{\varrho^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the only contribution to
s_{\varrho^{\prime}}
comes from
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to T(K)
, where
\tau
is the composition of
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to G(K)
and
G(K)\to T(K)
.

According to
4.9
, we can conclude that
s_{\varrho}
coincides with the reduction map
s_{\tau}:X\to S_{\tau}
of
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to T(K)
. This establishes the lemma.
∎

4.3.
Eyssidieux-Simpson Lefschetz theorem and its application

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
V\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})
be a
\mathbb{C}
-subspace. Let
a:X\to\mathcal{A}_{X}
be the Albanese morphism of
X
. Note that
a^{*}:H^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{X},\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{X}}^{1})\to H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})
is an isomorphism. Write
V^{\prime}:=(a^{*})^{-1}(V)
. Define
B(V)\subset\mathcal{A}_{X}
to be the largest abelian subvariety of
\mathcal{A}_{X}
such that
\eta|_{{B(V)}}=0
for every
\eta\in V^{\prime}
. Set
\mathcal{A}_{X,V}:=\mathcal{A}_{X}/B(V)
. The
partial Albanese morphism associated with
V
is the composition of
a
with the quotient map
\mathcal{A}_{X}\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
, denoted by
g_{V}:X\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
. Note that there exists
V_{0}\subset H^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{X,V},\Omega_{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}}^{1})
with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{0}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
such that
g_{V}^{*}V_{0}=V
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}}\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
be the universal covering and let
X_{V}
be
X\times_{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}}
. Note that
V_{0}
induces a natural linear map
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}}\to V_{0}^{*}
. Its composition with
X_{V}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}}
and
g_{V}^{*}:V_{0}\to V
gives rise to a holomorphic map

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{V}:X_{V}\to V^{*}.

(4.1)

Let
f:X\to S
be the Stein factorization of
g_{V}:X\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
with
q:S\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
the finite morphism. Set
\mathbb{V}:=q^{*}V_{0}
.

Definition 4.11
.

V
is called
perfect
if for any closed subvariety
Z\subset S
of dimension
d\geq 1
, one has
{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{d}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(Z,\Omega_{Z}^{d})]\neq 0
.

The terminology of “perfect
V
» in
4.11
is called “SSKB factorisable » in
[
Eys04
, Lemme 5.1.6]
.

Let us recall the following Lefschetz theorem by Eyssidieux, which is a generalization of previous work by Simpson
[
Sim92
]
. This theorem plays a crucial role in the proofs of
C
and
D
.

Theorem 4.12

(
[
Eys04
, Proposition 5.1.7]
)
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler normal space and let
V\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1})
be a subspace. Assume that

{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{\dim V}V\to H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{\dim V})]=\eta\neq 0.

Set
(\eta=0)=\cup_{i=1}^{k}Z_{k}
where
Z_{i}
are proper closed subvarieties of
X
. For each
Z_{i}
, denote by
V_{i}:={\rm Im}[V\to H^{0}(Z_{i},\Omega_{Z_{i}})]
. Assume that
V_{i}
is perfect for each
i
.
Then there are two possibilities which exclude each other :

—

either
V
is perfect ;

—

or for the holomorphic map
\widetilde{g}_{V}:X_{V}\to V^{*}
defined as (
4.1
),
(X_{V},\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))
is
1
-connected for any
t\in V^{*}
; i.e.
\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t)
is connected and
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))\to\pi_{1}(X_{V})
is surjective.

We need the following version of the Castelnuovo-De Franchis theorem proved by Catanese.

Theorem 4.13

(Castelnuovo-De Franchis-Catanese)
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler normal space and let
W\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
be the subspace of dimension
d\geq 2
such that

—

{\rm Im}\big(\Lambda^{d}W\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{d}_{X})\big)=0
;

—

for every hyperplane
W^{\prime}\subset W
,
{\rm Im}\big(\Lambda^{d-1}W^{\prime}\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{d-1}_{X})\big)\neq 0
.

Then there is a projective normal variety
S
of dimension
d-1
and a fibration
f:X\to S
such that
W\subset f^{*}H^{0}(S,\Omega_{S})
.

To apply
4.13
, we need to show the existence of a linear subspace
W\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
as in the theorem.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
X
be a projective normal variety and let
V\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
. Let
r
be the largest integer such that
{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{r}V\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{r}_{X})]\neq 0
. Assume that
r<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
.
There exists
W\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
such that

(i)

2\leq\dim W\leq r+1
.

(ii)

{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{\dim W}W\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{\dim W}_{X})]=0
;

(iii)

for every hyperplane
W^{\prime}\subsetneq W
, we always have
{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{\dim W-1}W^{\prime}\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{\dim W-1}_{X})]\neq 0
.

Démonstration.

By our assumption there exist
\{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r}\}\subset V
such that
\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{r}\neq 0
. Let
W_{0}\subset V
be the subspace generated by
\{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r}\}
. Since
r<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
, there exists
\omega\in V\setminus W_{0}
.

Pick a point
x\in X
such that
\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{r}(x)\neq 0
. Then there exists a coordinate system
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
x
such that
dz_{i}=\omega_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. Write
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(z)dz_{i}
. By our choice of
r
, we have

\omega_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega_{r}\wedge\omega=0.

It follows that

—

a_{j}(z)=0
for
j=r+1,\ldots,n
;

—

at least one of
a_{1}(z),\ldots,a_{r}(z)
is not constant.

Let
k+1
be the transcendental degree of
\{1,a_{1}(z),\ldots,a_{r}(z)\}\subset\mathbb{C}(U)
. Then
k\geq 1
. We assume that
1,a_{1}(z),\ldots,a_{k}(z)
is linearly independent for the transcendental extension
\mathbb{C}(U)/\mathbb{C}
. One can check by an easy linear algebra that the subspace
W
generated
\{\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{k},\omega\}
is an element of
E
. The lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
X
be a projective normal variety and let
V\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
. Let
r
be the largest integer such that
{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{r}V\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{r}_{X})]\neq 0
, which will be called
generic rank of
V
.
Consider the partial Albanese morphism
g_{V}:X\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
induced by
V
. Let
V_{0}\subset H^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{X,V},\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{X,V}})
be the linear subspace such that
g_{V}^{*}V_{0}=V
. Let
f:X\to S
be the Stein factorization of
g_{V}
with
q:S\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
the finite morphism. Consider
\mathbb{V}:=q^{*}V_{0}
. Assume that

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim Z}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(Z,\Omega_{Z}^{\dim Z})]\neq 0

for every proper closed subvariety
Z\subsetneq S
. Then there are two possibilities.

—

either

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim S}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(S,\Omega_{S}^{\dim S})]\neq 0;

—

or
r=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
.

Démonstration.

Assume that both

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim S}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(S,\Omega_{S}^{\dim S})]=0,

and
r<\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
. Therefore,
r<\dim S\leq\dim X
.
By
4.14
there is a subspace
W\subset V
with
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W=k+1\leq r+1
such that
{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{\dim W}W\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{\dim W}_{Y})]=0
, and for any subspace
W^{\prime}\subsetneq W
, we always have
{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{\dim W^{\prime}}W^{\prime}\to H^{0}(X,\Omega^{\dim W^{\prime}}_{X})]\neq 0
. By our assumption, we have
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W\leq\dim X
. By
4.13
, there is a fibration
p:X\to B
with
B
a projective normal variety with
\dim B=\dim W-1\leq\dim X-1
such that
W\subset p^{*}H^{0}(B,\Omega_{B}^{1})
. In particular, the generic rank of the forms in
W
is
\dim W-1
.
Consider the partial Albanese morphism
g_{W}:X\to\mathcal{A}_{X,W}
associated with
W
. We shall prove that
p
can be made as the Stein factorisation of
g_{W}
.

Note that each fiber of
p
is contracted by
g_{W}
. Therefore, we have a factorisation
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{X,W}
. Note that there exists a linear space
W_{0}\subset H^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{X,W},\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{X,W}})
such that
W=g_{W}^{*}W_{0}
. If
\dim h(B)<\dim B
, then the generic rank of
W
is less or equal to
\dim h(B)
. This contradicts with
4.13
. Therefore,
\dim h(B)=\dim B
. Let
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\prime}}}{{\to}}B^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}_{X,W}
be the Stein factorisation of
g_{W}
. Then there exists a birational morphism
\nu:B\to B^{\prime}
such that
p^{\prime}=\nu\circ p
. We can thus replace
B
by
B^{\prime}
, and
p
by
p^{\prime}
.

Recall that
f:X\to S
is the Stein factorisation of the partial Albanese morphism
g_{V}:X\to\mathcal{A}_{X,V}
associated with
V
. As
g_{W}
factors through the natural quotient map
\mathcal{A}_{X,V}\to\mathcal{A}_{X,W}
, it follows that
p:X\to B
factors through
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\to}}B
.

Assume that
\dim S=\dim B
. Then
\nu
is birational. Since
\dim B=\dim W-1
and the generic rank of
W
is
\dim W-1
, it follows that

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim S}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(S,\Omega_{S}^{\dim S})]\neq 0.

This contradicts with our assumption at the beginning. Hence
\dim S>\dim B
.

Let
Z
be a general fiber of
\nu
which is positive-dimensional. Since
W\subset p^{*}H^{0}(B,\Omega_{B}^{1})
, and we have assumed that the generic rank of
\mathbb{V}
is less than
\dim S
, it follows that the generic rank of
{\rm Im}\,[\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(Z,\Omega_{Z}^{1})]
is less than
\dim Z
. This implies that

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim Z}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(Z,\Omega_{Z}^{\dim Z})]=0,

which contradicts with our assumption. Therefore we obtain a contradiction. The lemma is proved.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Let
Y
be a normal projective variety. Let
{\bm{\varrho}}=\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,k}
be a family of reductive representations where
K_{i}
are non-archimedean local field. Let
\pi:X\to Y
be a Galois cover dominating all spectral covers induced by
\varrho_{i}
. Let
V\subset H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
be the set of all spectral forms (cf.
section

1.9
for definitions). We use the same notations as in
4.15
. Considering Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction maps
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}}:Y\to S_{\bm{\varrho}}
and
s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}:X\to S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
. One can check that, for every closed subvariety
Z\subset S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}^{\dim Z}\}\cdot Z>0
if and only if for any closed subvariety
W\subset S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
dominating
Z
under
\sigma_{\pi}:S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
defined in (
), one has

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim W}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(W,\Omega_{W}^{\dim W})]\neq 0.

In particular,
V
is perfect if and only if
\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}\}
is a Kähler class by
1.13
.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety and let
{\bm{\varrho}}:=\{\varrho_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,k}
be a family of reductive representations where
K_{i}
is a non-archimedean local field.
Let
s_{\bm{\varrho}}:X\to S_{\bm{\varrho}}
be the Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction map. Let
T_{\bm{\varrho}}
be the canonical
(1,1)
-current on
S_{\bm{\varrho}}
associated with
{\bm{\varrho}}
defined in
1.24
. Denote by
H_{i}
the Zariski closure of
\varrho_{i}(\pi_{1}(X))
. Assume that for any proper closed subvariety
\Sigma\subsetneq S_{\bm{\varrho}}
, one has
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
. Then

—

either
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim S_{\bm{\varrho}}}\cdot S_{\bm{\varrho}}>0
;

—

or the reduction map
s_{\sigma_{i}}:X\to S_{\sigma_{i}}
coincides with
s_{\varrho_{i}}:X\to S_{\varrho_{i}}
for each
i
, where
\sigma_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to(H_{i}/\mathcal{D}H_{i})(K_{i})
is the composition of
\varrho_{i}
with the group homomorphism
H_{i}\to H_{i}/\mathcal{D}H_{i}
.

Démonstration.

Assume that
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim S_{\bm{\varrho}}}\cdot S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}=0
. Let
Y\to X
be a Galois cover which dominates all spectral covers of
\varrho_{i}
. We pull back all the spectral one forms on
Y
to obtain a subspace
V\subset H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{1})
. Consider the partial Albanese morphism
g_{V}:Y\to\mathcal{A}_{Y,V}
associated to
V
, then
s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}:Y\to S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
is its Stein factorization with
q:S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}\to\mathcal{A}_{Y,V}
the finite morphism. Note that there is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear subspace
\mathbb{V}\subset H^{0}({S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}},\Omega^{1}_{S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}})
such that
s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}^{*}\mathbb{V}=V
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\bm{\varrho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\varrho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\varrho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\bm{\varrho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{i}}

Note that
\sigma_{\pi}
is finite surjective morphism. By
1.25
we have
T_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}=\sigma_{\pi}^{*}T_{{\bm{\varrho}}}
. By our assumption, for an proper closed subvariety
\Xi\subsetneq S_{\bm{\varrho}}
, one has
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim\Xi}\cdot\Xi>0
. Hence for an proper closed subvariety
\Xi\subsetneq S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
, one has
\{T_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}\}^{\dim\Xi}\cdot\Xi>0
. According to
4.16
, this implies that

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim\Xi}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(\Xi,\Omega_{\Xi}^{\dim\Xi})]\neq 0.

Since
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim S}\cdot S=0
, it follows that
\{T_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}\}^{\dim S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}\cdot S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}=0
. This implies that

{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{\dim S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}\mathbb{V}\to H^{0}(S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}},\Omega_{S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}^{\dim S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}})]=0.

Let
r
be the generic rank
V
. According to
4.16
, we have
r=\dim S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}-1
. By
4.15
, we have
r=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
. Therefore,
{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{r}V\to H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{r})]\simeq\mathbb{C}
.

Claim 4.18
.

For any non-zero
\eta\in{\rm Im}\,[\Lambda^{r}V\to H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{r})]
,
each irreducible component
Z^{\prime}
of
(\eta=0)
satisfies that
s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}(Z^{\prime})
is a proper subvariety of
S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
.

Démonstration.

Assume that this is not the case. Let
Z\to Z^{\prime}
be a desingularization. Set
V^{\prime}:={\rm Im}\,[V\to H^{0}(Z,\Omega_{Z}^{1})]
. Denote by
r^{\prime}
the generic rank of
V^{\prime}
. Then
r^{\prime}<r
as
Z^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
(\eta=0)
.
Write
\iota:Z\to Y
and
g:Z\to X
for the natural map. Then the Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction
s_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}:Z\to S_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
associated with
g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}
is the Stein factorization of the partial Albanese morphism
g_{V^{\prime}}:Z\to\mathcal{A}_{Z,V^{\prime}}
. We have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{{\bm{\varrho}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\bm{\varrho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{g}}

such that
\sigma_{\iota}
is a finite
surjective
morphism as we assume that
s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}(Z^{\prime})=S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
. Let
\Sigma\subsetneq S_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
be a proper closed subvariety. Let
\Sigma^{\prime}:=\sigma_{g}(\Sigma)
. Since
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim\Sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\Sigma^{\prime}>0
by our assumption, by
1.25

\{T_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
.
By
4.16
, it follows that the generic rank
r^{\prime}
of
V^{\prime}
is equal to
\dim S_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}-1=\dim S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}-1
. This contradicts with the fact that
r^{\prime}<r=\dim S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}-1
. The claim is proved. ∎

By the above claim,
s_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}(Z^{\prime})
is a proper subvariety of
S_{\pi^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}
.
Therefore, we have
\{T_{\bm{\varrho}}\}^{\dim S_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}}\cdot S_{g^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}>0
. Hence for each irreducible component
Z^{\prime}

of
(\eta=0)
,
{\rm Im}\,[V\to H^{0}(Z,\Omega_{Z}^{1})]
is perfect by
4.16
once again. We can apply
4.12
to conclude that for the holomorphic map
\widetilde{g}_{V}:Y_{V}\to V^{*}
defined as (
4.1
),
(Y_{V},\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))
is
1
-connected for any
t\in V^{*}
. For the covering
Y_{V}\to Y
, we know that
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Y_{V})\to\pi_{1}(Y)]
contains the derived subgroup
\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(Y)
of
\pi_{1}(Y)
. Then
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(Y_{V})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
contains
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}(\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(Y))
. On the other hand, since
(Y_{V},\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))
is
1
-connected for any
t\in V^{*}
, it follows that
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
contains
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}(\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(Y))
. Note that
V
is consists of all the spectral forms of
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}
for all
i
, hence each
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}
-equivariant harmonic mapping
u_{i}
vanishes over each connected component
p^{-1}(\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))
where
p:\widetilde{Y}\to Y
is the universal covering. Then
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}({\rm Im}\,[\pi_{1}(\widetilde{g}_{V}^{-1}(t))\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
fixes a point
P
in the Bruhat-Tits building, which implies that it is bounded. Therefore,
\pi^{*}\varrho_{i}(\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(Y))
is also bounded. Note that the image of
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(X)
.
Hence
\varrho_{i}(\mathcal{D}\pi_{1}(X))
is also bounded for each
\varrho_{i}
. The theorem then follows from
4.8
.
∎

4.4.
A factorization theorem

As an application of
4.17
, we will prove the following factorization theorem which partially generalizes previous theorem by Corlette-Simpson
[
CS08
]
. This result is also a warm-up for the proof of
4.22
.

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
X
be a projective normal variety and let
G
be an almost simple algebraic group defined over non-archimedean local field
K
of characteristic zero. Assume that
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to G(K)
is an unbounded, Zariski dense representation such that for any closed subvariety
Z
of
X
, the Zariski closure of
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z_{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is a semisimple algebraic group (including the finite group). Then after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover and a birational modification, there exists a fibration
g:X\to Y
, a representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(Y)\to G(K)
such that
g^{*}\tau=\varrho
, and
\dim Y\leq{\rm rank}_{K}G
.

Démonstration.

By
[
CDY22
, Proposition 2.5]
, after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover and a birational modification, there exists a fibration
g:X\to Y
over a smooth projective variety
Y
, and a big representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(Y)\to G(K)
such that we have
g^{*}\tau=\varrho
. Since
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is Zariski dense, it follows that the Zariski closure
G^{\prime}
of
\tau(\pi_{1}(Y))
contains the identity component of
G
, which is also almost simple. We replace
G^{\prime}
by
G
and thus
\tau
is Zariski dense. Let
s_{\tau}:Y\to S_{\tau}
be the Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction of
\tau
.

Claim 4.20
.

The
(1,1)
-class
\{T_{\tau}\}
on
S_{\tau}
is Kähler, where
T_{\tau}
is the canonical current on
S_{\tau}
associated to
\tau
.

Démonstration.

By
1.13
, it is equivalent to prove that for any closed subvariety
\Sigma\subset S_{\tau}
,
\int_{\Sigma}\{T_{\tau}\}^{\dim\Sigma}>0
. We will prove it by induction on
\dim\Sigma
.

Induction
. Assume that for every closed subvariety
\Sigma\subset S_{\tau}
of dimension
\leq r-1
,
\{T_{\tau}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
.

Let
\Sigma
be any closed subvariety of
S_{\tau}
with
\dim\Sigma=r
. Let
Z
be a desingularization of any irreducible component in
s_{\tau}^{-1}(\Sigma)
which is surjective over
\Sigma
. Denote by
f:Z\to Y
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{f^{*}\tau}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f^{*}\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{f}}

By
1.25
,
\sigma_{f}
is a finite morphism whose image is
\Sigma
and
T_{f^{*}\tau}=\sigma_{f}^{*}T_{\tau}
.

We first prove the induction for
\dim\Sigma=1
. In this case
\dim S_{f^{*}\tau}=1
. Since the spectral forms associated to
f^{*}\tau
are not constant, it follows that
T_{f^{*}\tau}
is big. By
1.25
,
\{T_{\tau}|_{\Sigma}\}
is big. Therefore, we prove the induction when
\dim\Sigma=1
.

Assume now the induction holds for closed subvariety
\Sigma\subset S_{\tau}
with
\dim\Sigma\leq r-1
. Let us treat the case
\dim\Sigma=r
. By
1.25
and the induction, we know that for any closed proper positive dimensional subvariety
\Xi\subset S_{f^{*}\tau}
, we have
\{T_{f^{*}\tau}\}^{\dim\Xi}\cdot\Xi>0
. Note that the conditions in
4.17
for
f^{*}\tau
is fulfilled. Therefore, there are two possibilities :

—

either
\{T_{f^{*}\tau}\}^{r}\cdot S_{f^{*}\tau}>0
;

—

or the reduction map
s_{f^{*}\tau}:Z\to S_{f^{*}\tau}
coincides with
s_{\nu}:Z\to S_{\nu}
, where
\nu:\pi_{1}(Z)\to(H/\mathcal{D}H)(K)
is the composition of
\tau
with the group homomorphism
H\to H/\mathcal{D}H
. Here
H
is the Zariski closure of
f^{*}\tau
.

If the first case happens, by
1.25
again we have
\int_{\Sigma}\{T_{\tau}\}^{\dim\Sigma}>0
. we finish the proof of the induction for
\Sigma\subset S_{\tau}
with
\dim\Sigma=r
. Assume that the second situation occurs. Since
H
is assumed to be semisimple, it follows that
H/\mathcal{D}H
finite. Therefore,
\nu
is bounded and thus
S_{f^{*}\tau}
is a point. This contradicts with the fact that
\dim S_{f^{*}\tau}=\dim\Sigma=r>0
. Therefore, the second situation cannot occur. We finish the proof of the induction. The claim is proved.
∎

This claim in particular implies that

(
\diamondsuit
)

the
generic rank

r
of the multivalued holomorphic 1-forms on
Y
induced by the differential of harmonic mappings of
\tau
is equal to
\dim S_{\tau}
.

Since
G
is almost simple, by
[
CDY22
, Theorem 6.1]
we know that the Katzarkov-Eyssidieux reduction map
s_{\tau}:Y\to S_{\tau}
is birational.
Therefore
r=\dim Y
. On the other hand, we note that
r
is less than or equal to the dimension of the Bruhat-Tits building
\Delta(G)_{K}
, which is equal to
{\rm rank}_{K}G
. It follows that
\dim Y\leq{\rm rank}\,_{K}G
. The theorem is proved.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

The authors do not know the proof of
(\diamondsuit)
which does not go through the stronger conclusion
4.20
.
In
[
Zuo96
, p. 148]
, Zuo claimed
(\diamondsuit)
for much weaker assumption for
\varrho
without providing a proof. Using our terminology, it can be stated as follows :

(
\spadesuit
)

let
X
,
G
and
K
be as in
4.19
. If
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to G(K)
is a Zariski dense, unbounded, and big representation, then the generic rank of the multivalued form equals to
\dim X
.

Zuo’s claim is essential in his proof of the result that such
X
is of general type. While we cannot provide a counter-example to Claim
(\spadesuit)
, we believe his statement is too strong. Indeed, as we will see in next subsection, we have to apply
4.5
to add sufficiently many new unbounded representations of
\pi_{1}(X)
into non-archimedean local fields such that the collection of all the induced multivalued forms have the rank equal to
\dim X
.

A new proof, along with a much more general statement on the hyperbolicity of
X
, can be found in
[
CDY22
, Theorem I]
. We emphasize that in
[
CDY22
]
we avoid the use of Claim
(\spadesuit)
, and use a completely different method.

4.5.
Constructing Kähler classes via representations into non-archimedean fields

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. In this subsection we will prove a more general theorem than
4.5
.

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
. Then there is a family of representations
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,M}
where
K_{i}
are non-archimedean local fields such that

—

For each
i=1,\ldots,M
,
[\tau_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}(K_{i})
;

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
of
{\bm{\tau}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
defined in
3.1
.

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
defined over
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

To prove this theorem, we need several preparations.
Let
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(K)
be a reductive representation, where
K
is a non-archimedian local field of characteristic zero.
Then we get the spectral covering
\pi:X^{\!\rm sp}\to X
, the spectral one forms
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{l}\in H^{0}(X^{\!\rm sp},\pi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X})
, the reduction map
s_{\tau}:X\to S_{\tau}
and the canonical current
T_{\tau}
on
S_{\tau}
(cf.
1.23
).

We define a Zariski closed set
W_{\tau}\subset TX
as follows.
Each spectral one form
\eta_{i}\in H^{0}(X^{\!\rm sp},\pi^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X})
determines a section of
H^{0}(\pi^{*}TX,\mathcal{O}_{\pi^{*}TX})
, where
\pi^{*}TX=TX\times_{X}X^{\!\rm sp}
.
Hence we get the zero locus
\{\eta_{i}=0\}\subset\pi^{*}TX
.
We set
W_{\tau}^{\prime}=\cap_{i=1}^{l}\{\eta_{i}=0\}
.
Then
W_{\tau}^{\prime}\subset\pi^{*}TX
is Zariski closed.
We set
W_{\tau}=p(W_{\tau}^{\prime})
.
Since the natural map
p:\pi^{*}TX\to TX
is finite,
W_{\tau}
is Zariski closed.
Note that the set
\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{l}\}
is invariant under the action of the Galois group
\mathrm{Gal}(X^{\!\rm sp}/X)
.
Hence we have

p^{-1}(W_{\tau})=W_{\tau}^{\prime}.

(4.2)

Let
f:Z\to X
be a morphism from another smooth projective variety
Z
.
Then we get
W_{f^{*}\tau}\subset TZ
from
f^{*}\tau:\pi_{1}(Z)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(K)
.

Lemma 4.23
.

Under the map
f_{*}:TZ\to TX
, we have
W_{f^{*}\tau}=(f_{*})^{-1}W_{\tau}
.

Démonstration.

We have a natural map
Z^{\!\rm sp}\to X^{\!\rm sp}
induced from
f:Z\to X
.
This induces the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Z}^{*}TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

Let
\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\}\subset H^{0}(\pi_{Z}^{*}TZ,\mathcal{O}_{\pi_{Z}^{*}TZ})
be the set of spectral one forms defined by
f^{*}\tau:\pi_{1}(Z)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(K)
.
Then we have
\{\eta_{1}\circ g,\ldots,\eta_{l}\circ g\}=\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}\}
as sets.
Hence
g^{-1}W^{\prime}_{\tau}=W^{\prime}_{f^{*}\tau}
.
By (
), we have
p_{Z}^{-1}(f_{*})^{-1}W_{\tau}=W^{\prime}_{f^{*}\tau}
.
Since
\pi_{Z}:Z^{\!\rm sp}\to Z
is surjective,
p_{Z}
is also surjective.
Hence
W_{f^{*}\tau}=p_{Z}(W^{\prime}_{f^{*}\tau})=(f_{*})^{-1}W_{\tau}
.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
.
Let
{\bm{\tau}}=\{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\}
be a set of reductive representations over non-archimedian local fields of characteristic zero such that
[\tau_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}
.
Then we get
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
and
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
(cf.
1.24
).
We define
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}\subset TX
by
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}=\cap_{i=1}^{k}W_{\tau_{i}}
.

Corollary 4.24
.

Let
f:Z\to X
be a morphism of smooth projective varieties.
Then we have
(f_{*})^{-1}(W_{{\bm{\tau}}})=W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
under the map
f_{*}:TZ\to TX
.

Démonstration.

This follows directly from
4.23
.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

Assume that
\dim S_{{\bm{\tau}}}=\dim X
.
Then
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim S_{{\bm{\tau}}}}\cdot S_{{\bm{\tau}}}>0
if and only if
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}\cap T_{x}X=\{0\}
for generic points
x\in X
.

Démonstration.

Let
\pi:Y\to X
be a Galois cover dominating all spectral covers induced by
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{l}
.
Let
V\subset H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y})
be the set of all spectral forms.
We have
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim S_{{\bm{\tau}}}}\cdot S_{{\bm{\tau}}}>0

if and only if

{\rm Im}[\Lambda^{\dim Y}V\to H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y}^{\dim Y})]\neq 0.

This is true if and only if for generic smooth points
y\in Y
, the induced map
V\to(T_{y}Y)^{*}
is surjective, which is equivalent to
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}\cap T_{x}X=\{0\}
for generic points
x\in X
.
∎

We say
{\bm{\tau}}
is
full with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
if the following holds :

—

s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
,

—

W_{{\bm{\tau}}}
is
minimal
, i.e., for every reductive representation
\sigma
such that
[\sigma]\in\mathfrak{C}
, we have
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}\subset W_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Assume that
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
and that
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.
Then for every
{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}
which is full with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Démonstration.

Let
\Sigma\subset S_{\mathfrak{C}}
be a closed subvariety.
We shall show
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
.
Let
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset X
be a closed subvariety such that
\dim\Sigma^{\prime}=\dim\Sigma
and
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}(\Sigma^{\prime})=\Sigma
.
Let
Z\to\Sigma^{\prime}
be a desingularization and let
f:Z\to X
be the induced map.
We consider
s_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}:Z\to S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
.
Since
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class, we have
\{T_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}}\cdot S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}>0
(cf.
1.25
).
By
4.25
, we have
W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}\cap T_{x}Z=\{0\}
for generic points
x\in Z
.

Since
{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}
is full, we have
W_{{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\subset W_{{\bm{\tau}}}
.
Hence by
4.24
, we have
W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\subset W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
.
Hence we have
W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\cap T_{x}Z=\{0\}
for generic points
x\in Z
.
Hence by
4.25
, we have
\{T_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\}^{\dim S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}}\cdot S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}>0
.
Thus by
1.25
, we have
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}^{\prime}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
{\bm{\tau}}=\{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\}
be full with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
.
Suppose
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
for every proper closed subvariety
\Sigma\subsetneqq S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
.
Then
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Démonstration.

We will apply
4.17
.
We have two possibilities.
Assume that the first case occurs.
In this case, we have
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim S_{{\bm{\tau}}}}\cdot S_{{\bm{\tau}}}>0
as desired and by
1.13
,
\{T_{\bm{\tau}}\}
is Kähler.
Therefore, in the following, we assume that the second case occurs.
In this case, we apply
4.5
.

Let us denote by
H_{i}
the Zariski closure of
\tau_{i}(\pi_{1}(X))
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, which is a reductive algebraic group over
L_{i}
.
By
[
uh
]
, there is some number field
k_{i}
and some non-archimedean place
v_{i}
of
k_{i}
such that
L_{i}=(k_{i})_{v_{i}}
and
H_{i}
is defined over
k_{i}
.
Denote
T_{i}:=H_{i}/\mathcal{D}H_{i}
, which is a tori. Replacing
k_{i}
by a finite extension, we may assume that
T_{i}
is defined over
k_{i}
.
Then by
4.17
,
s_{\tau_{i}}:X\to S_{\tau_{i}}
coincides with
s_{\sigma_{i}}:X\to S_{\sigma_{i}}
, where
\sigma_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to T_{i}(L_{i})
is the composition of
\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to H_{i}(L_{i})
with the group homomorphism
H_{i}\to T_{i}
.
Consider the morphisms of affine
k_{i}
-schemes of finite type

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm B}(X,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm B}(X,T_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\rm B}(X,H_{i})}

(4.3)

Then by
1.20
,
\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
is transferred via the diagram (
4.3
) to some absolutely constructible subset
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
of
M_{\rm B}(X,T_{i})
.
Consider the reduction map
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
defined in
3.1
.
Let
f:X\to S
be the Stein factorisation of
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times s_{\mathfrak{C}_{k}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\mathfrak{C}_{k}}
.

Claim 4.28
.

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
coincides with
f:X\to S
.

Démonstration.

Note that the reduction map
s_{\sigma_{i}}:X\to S_{\sigma_{i}}
coincides with
s_{\tau_{i}}:X\to S_{\tau_{i}}
.
By (
4.3
) and the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
,
[\sigma_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(L_{i})
.
Therefore,
s_{\sigma_{i}}
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
.
Hence
s_{\tau_{i}}:X\to S_{\tau_{i}}
factors through
X\to S
.
Thus
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}
factors through
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\to}}S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
.
By the construction of
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
, the map
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
factors through
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
.
By
S_{\mathfrak{C}}=S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
, we have
X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}\to S
.
∎

Since
T_{i}
are all algebraic tori defined over number fields
k_{i}
, we apply
4.5
to conclude that there exists a family of reductive representations
{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}:=\{\varrho_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to T_{i}(K_{ij})\}_{j=1,\ldots,n_{i}}
with
K_{ij}
non-archimedean local field such that

(1)

For each
i=1,\ldots,k;j=1,\ldots,n_{i}
,
[\varrho_{ij}]\in\mathfrak{C}_{i}(K_{ij})
;

(2)

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
of
{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
;

(3)

for the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
over
S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}
associated with
{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}\}
is a Kähler class.

By the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{i}
, there exist a finite extension
F_{ij}
of
K_{ij}
and reductive representations
\{\delta_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(F_{ij})\}_{j=1,\ldots,n_{i}}
such that

(a)

For each
i=1,\ldots,k;j=1,\ldots,n_{i}
,
[\delta_{ij}]\in\mathfrak{C}(F_{ij})
;

(b)

the Zariski closure of
\delta_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(F_{ij})
is contained in
H_{i}
;

(c)

[\eta_{ij}]=[\varrho_{ij}]\in M_{\rm B}(X,T_{i})(F_{ij})
, where
\eta_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to T_{i}(F_{ij})
is the composition of
\delta_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to H_{i}(F_{ij})
with the group homomorphism
H_{i}\to T_{i}
.

Therefore,
\eta_{ij}
is conjugate to
\varrho_{ij}
and thus their reduction map coincides.
We have the factorization
X\to S_{\delta_{ij}}\overset{q_{ij}}{\to}S_{\eta_{ij}}
.
By
1.24
, one can see that

\displaystyle q_{ij}^{*}T_{\varrho_{ij}}=q_{ij}^{*}T_{\eta_{ij}}\leq T_{\delta_{ij}}.

(4.4)

Consider the family of representations
\bm{\delta}:=\{\delta_{ij}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(F_{ij})\}_{i=1,\ldots,k;j=1,\ldots,n_{i}}
.
Let
e_{i}:S\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{i}}=S_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}
be the natural map. Note that
e_{1}\times\cdots\times e_{k}:S\to S_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\mathfrak{C}_{k}}
is finite.
By
Items

(2)
and
(3)
,
\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_{i}^{*}T_{{\bm{\varrho}}_{i}}\}
is Kähler on
S=S_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
By (
4.4
), we conclude that
\{T_{\bm{\delta}}\}
is Kähler on
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
. According to
4.26
, it implies that
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is Kähler on
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
f:Z\to X
be a morphism of smooth projective varieties.
Let
j:M_{\rm B}(X,N)\to M_{\rm B}(Z,N)
be the induced map.
If
{\bm{\tau}}
is full with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
, then
f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}
is full with respect to
j(\mathfrak{C})
.

Démonstration.

Note that
s_{j(\mathfrak{C})}:Z\to S_{j(\mathfrak{C})}
is the Stein factorization of the composite of
Z\to X
and
X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
Similarly,
s_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}:Z\to S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
is the Stein factorization of the composite of
Z\to X
and
X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
.
Hence
s_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}:Z\to S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
coincides with
s_{j(\mathfrak{C})}:Z\to S_{j(\mathfrak{C})}
.

Let
\rho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a reductive representation such that
[\rho]\in\mathfrak{C}
.
Then
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}\subset W_{\rho}
.
We have
(f_{*})^{-1}(W_{\rho})=W_{f^{*}\rho}
(cf.
4.23
) and
(f_{*})^{-1}(W_{{\bm{\tau}}})=W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
(cf.
4.24
) under the map
f_{*}:TZ\to TX
.
This shows that
W_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
is minimal.
∎

Lemma 4.30
.

Suppose
{\bm{\tau}}
is full with respect to
\mathfrak{C}
.
Then
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Démonstration.

Let
\Sigma\subset S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
be a closed subvariety.
It suffices to show
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
by
1.13
.
We prove this by Noetherian induction.
Hence we assume that
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim\Sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\Sigma^{\prime}>0
for every proper closed subvarieties
\Sigma^{\prime}\subsetneqq\Sigma
.

We take a subvariety
Z\subset X
such that
\dim Z=\dim\Sigma
and
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}(Z)=\Sigma
.
Let
Z^{\prime}\to Z
be a desingularization.
Let
f:Z^{\prime}\to X
be the induced map.
Then by
4.29
,
f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}
is full with respect to
j(\mathfrak{C})
, where
j:M_{\rm B}(X,N)\to M_{\rm B}(Z,N)
is the induced map.
Note that
j(\mathfrak{C})
is an absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(Z^{\prime},N)(\mathbb{C})
.
By our hypothesis of Noetherian induction and
1.25
,
f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}
satisfies the assumption of
4.17
; i.e., for every proper closed subvariety
\Sigma^{\prime}\subsetneqq S_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}
, we have
\{T_{f^{*}{\bm{\varrho}}}\}^{\dim\Sigma^{\prime}}\cdot\Sigma^{\prime}>0
.
Thus by
4.27
,
\{T_{f^{*}{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.
Hence
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}^{\dim\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma>0
as desired (cf.
1.25
).
This completes the induction step.
∎

Proof of
4.22
.

By
3.1
and
1.28
, there are non-archimedean local fields
L_{1},\ldots,L_{\ell}
of characteristic zero and reductive representations
\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(L_{i})
such that
[\tau_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}(L_{i})
and
s_{\mathfrak{C}}:X\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}
is the Stein factorization of
(s_{\tau_{1}},\ldots,s_{\tau_{\ell}}):X\to S_{\tau_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\tau_{\ell}}
.
Write
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}
.
By the Noetherian property, we may assume that
W_{{\bm{\tau}}}
is minimal.
Hence
{\bm{\tau}}
is full.
By
4.30
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.
∎

4.6.
Holomorphic convexity of Galois coverings

In this subsection we will prove
C
. We shall use the notations and results proven in
sections

3.4
and
3.29
without recalling the details.

Theorem 4.31
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
defined in
1.17
. Assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is defined on
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\pi:\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}\to X
be the covering corresponding to the group
\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho\subset\pi_{1}(X)
where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representations such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. Then
\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is holomorphically convex. In particular, if
\pi_{1}(X)
is a subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
whose Zariski closure is reductive, then
\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is holomorphically convex.

Démonstration.

Let
H:=\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho\cap\sigma
, where
\sigma
is the
\mathbb{C}
-VHS defined in
proposition

3.13
and
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}
. Denote by
\widetilde{X}_{H}:=\widetilde{X}/H
. Let
\mathscr{D}
be the period domain associated to the
\mathbb{C}
-VHS
\sigma
defined in
proposition

3.13
and let
p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
be the period mapping. By (
3.5
),
H=\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho
, where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representation such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}
. Therefore,
\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\widetilde{X}_{H}
.

Consider the product

\Psi=s_{\mathfrak{C}}\circ\pi_{H}\times p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}

where
p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
is the period mapping of
\sigma
. Recall that
\Psi
factors through a proper surjective fibration
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
. Moreover, there is a properly discontinuous action of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
such that
{\rm sh}_{H}
is equivariant with respect to this action.
Write
g:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
to be the induced holomorphic map. Denote by
\phi:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
the composition of
g
and the projection map
S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{D}
. Since the period mapping
p
is horizontal, and
{\rm sh}_{H}
is surjective, it follows that
\phi
is also horizontal.

Recall that in
3.38
we prove that
there is a finite index normal subgroup
N
of
\pi_{1}(X)/H
and a homomorphism
\nu:N\to{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{S}_{H})
such that
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is
\nu
-equivariant and
\nu(N)
acts on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
properly discontinuous and without fixed point.
Let
Y:=\widetilde{X}_{H}/N
. Moreover,
c:Y\to X
is a finite Galois étale cover and
N
gives rise to a proper surjective fibration

Y\to\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
between compact normal complex spaces. Write
W:=\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
. Then
\widetilde{S}_{H}\to W
is a topological Galois unramified covering.
Recall that the canonical bundle
K_{\mathscr{D}}
on the period domain
\mathscr{D}
can be endowed with a
G_{0}
-invariant smooth metric
h_{\mathscr{D}}
whose curvature is strictly positive-definite in the horizontal direction. As
\phi:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
is
\nu(N)
-equivariant, it follows that
\phi^{*}K_{\mathscr{D}}
descends to a line bundle on the quotient
W:=\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
, denoted by
L_{G}
. The smooth metric
h_{\mathscr{D}}
induces a smooth metric on
L_{G}
whose curvature form is denoted by
T
. Let
x\in W
be a smooth point of
W
and let
v\in T_{\widetilde{S}_{H},x}
. Then
|v|_{\omega}^{2}>0
if
d\phi(v)\neq 0
.

We fix a reference point
x_{0}
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
. Let
\mathscr{S}
be the symmetric space associated with
\mathscr{D}
endowed with the natural metric
d_{\mathscr{S}}
. Then there exists natural quotient map
\mathscr{D}\to\mathscr{S}
. It induces a
\nu(N)
-equivariant pluri-harmonic mapping
u:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\mathscr{S}
. Define
\phi_{0}:=2d^{2}_{\mathscr{S}}(u(x),u(x_{0}))
. By
[
Eys04
, Proposition 3.3.2]
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\phi_{0}\geq\omega=q^{*}T,

(4.5)

where
q:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}/\nu(N)
denotes the quotient map.

We now apply
4.22
to find a family of representations
{\bm{\tau}}:=\{\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(K_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
where
K_{i}
are non-archimedean local fields such that

—

For each
i=1,\ldots,m
,
[\tau_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}(K_{i})
;

—

The reduction map
s_{{\bm{\tau}}}:X\to S_{{\bm{\tau}}}
of
{\bm{\tau}}
coincides with
s_{\mathfrak{C}}
.

—

For the canonical current
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
defined over
S_{\mathfrak{C}}
,
\{T_{{\bm{\tau}}}\}
is a Kähler class.

Consider

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\mathfrak{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\bm{\tau}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\tau_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\bm{\tau}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\tau_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}

Note that
p
is a finite surjective morphism.

We fix a reference point
x_{0}
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
. For each
i=1,\ldots,m
, let
u_{i}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\Delta({\rm GL}_{N})_{K_{i}}
be the
\tau_{i}
-equivariant harmonic mapping from
\widetilde{X}_{H}
to the Bruhat-Tits building of
{\rm GL}_{N}(K_{i})
whose existence was ensured by a theorem of Gromov-Schoen
[
GS92
]
. Then the function
\tilde{\phi}_{i}(x):=2d_{i}^{2}(u_{i}(x),u_{i}(x_{0}))
defined over
\widetilde{X}_{H}
is locally Lipschitz, where
d_{i}:\Delta({\rm GL}_{N})_{K_{i}}\times\Delta({\rm GL}_{N})_{K_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the distance function on the Bruhat-Tits building. By
proposition

1.26
, it induces a continuous psh functions
\{\phi_{i}:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}
such that
{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\phi_{i}\geq r_{i}^{*}T_{\tau_{i}}

for each
i
. By the definition of
T_{{\bm{\tau}}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\phi_{i}\geq r^{*}T_{{\bm{\tau}}}.

(4.6)

Therefore, putting (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) together we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\phi_{i}\geq q^{*}(f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T).

(4.7)

As
f
is a finite surjective morphism,
\{f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}\}
is also Kähler by
1.13
.

By
3.39
, we know that
g:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to S_{\mathfrak{C}}\times\mathscr{D}
has discrete fibers. Since
T
is induced by the curvature form of
(K_{\mathscr{D}},h_{\mathscr{D}})
, and
\phi:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to\mathscr{D}
is horizontal, we can prove that for every irreducible positive dimensional closed subvariety
Z
of
W
,
f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T
is strictly positive at general smooth points of
Z
. Therefore,

\{f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T\}^{\dim Z}\cdot Z=\int_{Z}(f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T)^{\dim Z}>0.

Recall that
W
is projective by the proof of
3.40
. We utilize
1.13
to conclude that
\{f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T\}
is Kähler.

Given that
\widetilde{S}_{H}\to W
represents a topological Galois unramified cover, we can apply
proposition

1.14
in conjunction with (
4.7
) to deduce that
\widetilde{S}_{H}
is a Stein manifold. Furthermore, since
\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is a proper surjective holomorphic fibration, the holomorphic convexity of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
follows from the Cartan-Remmert theorem. Ultimately, the theorem is established by noting that
\widetilde{X}_{H}=\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}
.
∎

4.7.
Steiness of infinite Galois coverings

We shall use the notations in the proof of
4.31
without recalling their definitions.

Theorem 4.32
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety. Consider an absolutely constructible subset
\mathfrak{C}
of
M_{\rm B}(X,{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
as defined in
1.17
. We further assume that
\mathfrak{C}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. If
\mathfrak{C}
is considered to be
large
, meaning that for any closed subvariety
Z
of
X
, there exists a reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}
and
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is infinite, then all intermediate coverings between
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}
of
X
are Stein manifolds.

Démonstration.

Note that
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is a proper holomorphic surjective fibration.

Claim 4.33
.

{\rm sh}_{H}
is biholomorphic.

Démonstration.

Assume that there exists a positive-dimensional compact subvariety
Z
of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
which is contained in some fiber of
{\rm sh}_{H}
. Consider
W:=\pi_{H}(Z)
which is a compact positive-dimensional irreducible subvariety of
X
. Therefore,
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})]
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})
. By the definition of
\widetilde{X}_{H}
, for any reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
, we have
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])=\{1\}
. Therefore,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])=\{1\}
is finite. This contradicts with out assumption that
\mathfrak{C}
is large. Hence,
{\rm sh}_{H}
is a one-to-one proper holomorphic map of complex normal spaces. Consequently, it is biholomorphic.
∎

By the proof of
4.31
, there exist

—

a topological Galois unramified covering
q:\widetilde{X}_{H}=\widetilde{S}_{H}\to W
, where
W
is a projective normal variety ;

—

a positive
(1,1)
-current with continuous potential
f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T
over
W
such that
\{f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T\}
is Kähler ;

—

a continuous semi-positive plurisubharmonic function
\sum_{i=0}^{m}\phi_{i}
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
such that we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\phi_{i}\geq q^{*}(f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T).

(4.8)

Let
p:\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{X}_{H}
be the intermediate Galois covering of
X
between
\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}_{H}
. By (
4.5
) we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\sum_{i=0}^{m}p^{*}\phi_{i}\geq(q\circ p)^{*}(f^{*}T_{\bm{\tau}}+T).

(4.9)

We apply
proposition

1.14
to conclude that
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
is Stein.
∎

Annexe A
Shafarevich conjecture for projective normal varieties

Ya Deng, Ludmil Katzarkov
a
a

a

E-mail:
l.katzarkov@miami.edu
, University of Miami, Coral Gables, FL, USA; Institute of Mathematics
and Informatics, Bulgarian Academy of Sciences, Sofia, Bulgaria; NRU HSE, Moscow, Russia
& Katsutoshi Yamanoi

In this appendix, we aim to extend
4.31
and
4.32
to include singular normal varieties, and thus completing the proofs of
C
and
D
.

A.1.
Absolutely constructible subset (II)

Let
X
be a projective normal variety.
Following the recent work of Lerer
[
Ler22
]
, we can also define absolutely constructible subsets in the character variety
M_{\rm B}(X,N):=M_{\rm B}(\pi_{1}(X),{\rm GL}_{N})
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
X
be a normal projective variety,
\mu:Y\to X
be a resolution of singularities, and
\iota:M_{\rm B}(X,N)\hookrightarrow M_{\rm B}(Y,N)
be the embedding. A subset
\mathfrak{C}\subset M_{\rm B}(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
is called
absolutely constructible
if
\iota(\mathfrak{C})
is an absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(Y,N)
in the sense of
1.17
.

Note that the above definition does not depend on the choice of the resolution of singularities (cf.
[
Ler22
, Lemma 2.7]
). Moreover, we have the following result.

Proposition A.2

(
[
Ler22
, Proposition 2.8]
)
.

Let
X
be a normal projective variety. Then
M_{\rm B}(X,N)
is absolutely constructible in the sense of
A.1
.

This result holds significant importance, as it provides a fundamental example of absolutely constructible subsets for projective normal varieties. It is worth noting that in
[
Ler22
, Proposition 2.8]
, it is explicitly stated that
\iota(M_{\rm B}(X,N))
is
U(1)
-invariant, with
\iota
defined in
A.1
. However, it should be emphasized that the proof can be easily adapted to show
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariance, similar to the approach used in the proof of
proposition

3.44
.

A.2.
Reductive Shafarevich conjecture for projective normal varieties

Theorem A.3
.

Let
Y
be a projective normal variety. Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(Y,N)(\mathbb{C})
, defined on
\mathbb{Q}
(e.g.
\mathfrak{C}=M_{\rm B}(Y,N)
). Consider the covering
\pi:\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}\to Y
corresponding to the subgroup
\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho
of
\pi_{1}(Y)
, where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representations such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}
. Then the complex space
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is holomorphically convex. In particular,

—

The covering corresponding to the intersection of the kernels of all reductive representations of

\pi_{1}(Y)
in
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is holomorphically convex ;

—

if
\pi_{1}(Y)
is a subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
whose Zariski closure is reductive, then the universal covering of
Y
is holomorphically convex.

Démonstration.

Let
\mu:X\to Y
be any desingularization. Let
j:M_{\rm B}(Y,N)\hookrightarrow M_{\rm B}(X,N)
the closed immersion induced by
\mu
, which is a morphism of affine
\mathbb{Q}
-schemes of finite type . Then by
A.1
,
j(\mathfrak{C})
is an absolutely constructible in the sense of
1.17
. Since
\mathfrak{C}
is defined on
\mathbb{Q}
, so is
j(\mathfrak{C})
. We shall use the notations in
3.29
. Let
\widetilde{X}_{H}
be the covering associated with the subgroup
H:=\cap_{\varrho}\ker\varrho
of
\pi_{1}(X)
where
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representations such that
[\varrho]\in j(\mathfrak{C})(\mathbb{C})
. In other words,
H:=\cap_{\tau}\ker\mu^{*}\tau
where
\tau:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representations such that
[\tau]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. Denote by
H_{0}:=\cap_{\tau}\ker\tau
where
\tau:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
ranges over all reductive representations such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
. Therefore,
H=(\mu_{*})^{-1}(H_{0})
, where
\mu_{*}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(Y)
is a surjective homeomorphism as
Y
is normal. Therefore, the natural homeomorphism
\pi_{1}(X)/H\to\pi_{1}(Y)/H_{0}
is an isomorphism. Then
\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\widetilde{X}/H
and
\widetilde{Y}_{H}:=\widetilde{Y}/H_{0}
where
\widetilde{X}
(resp.
\widetilde{Y}
) is the universal covering of
X
(resp.
X
). It induces a lift
p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Claim A.4
.

p:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is a proper surjective holomorphic fibration with connected fibers.

Démonstration.

Note that
{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{X}_{H}/X)=\pi_{1}(X)/H\simeq\pi_{1}(Y)/H_{0}={\rm Aut}(\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}/Y)
.
Therefore,
\widetilde{X}_{H}
is the base change
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}\times_{Y}X
. Note that each fiber of
\mu
is connected as
Y
is normal. It follows that each fiber of
p
is connected. The claim is proved.
∎

By
3.29
, we know that there exist a proper surjective holomorphic fibration
{\rm sh}_{H}:\widetilde{X}_{H}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
such that

\widetilde{S}_{H}
is a Stein space. Therefore, for each connected compact subvariety
Z\subset\widetilde{X}_{H}
,
{\rm sh}_{H}(Z)
is a point. By
A.4
, it follows that each fiber of
p
is compact and connected, and thus is contracted by
{\rm sh}_{H}
. Therefore,
{\rm sh}_{H}
factors through a proper surjective fibration
f:\widetilde{X}_{\mathfrak{C}}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Therefore,
f
is a proper surjective holomorphic fibration over a Stein space. By the Cartan-Remmert theorem,
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is holomorphically convex.

If we define
\mathfrak{C}
as
M_{\rm B}(Y,N)
, then according to
Proposition

A.2
,
\mathfrak{C}
is also absolutely constructible. As a result, the last two claims can be deduced. Thus, the theorem is proven.
∎

Theorem A.5
.

Let
Y
be a projective normal variety. Let
\mathfrak{C}
be an absolutely constructible subset of
M_{\rm B}(Y,N)(\mathbb{C})
, defined on
\mathbb{Q}
(e.g.
\mathfrak{C}=M_{\rm B}(Y,N)
). Let
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
be
large
in the sense that for any closed positive dimensional subvariety
Z
of
Y
, there exists a reductive representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
such that
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
and
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is infinite. Then all intermediate Galois coverings of
Y
between
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
are Stein spaces. Here
\widetilde{Y}
denotes the universal covering of
Y
.

Démonstration.

Let
\mu:X\to Y
be any desingularization. In the following, we will use the same notations as in the proof of
A.3
without explicitly recalling their definitions. Recall that we have constructed three proper surjective holomorphic fibrations
p
,
f
, and
{\rm sh}_{H}
satisfying the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{S}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm sh}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Claim A.6
.

f:\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}\to\widetilde{S}_{H}
is a biholomorphism.

The proof follows a similar argument to that of
4.33
. For the sake of completeness, we will provide it here.

Proof of
A.6
.

As each fibers of
f
is compact and connected, it suffices to prove that there are no compact positive dimensional subvarieties
Z
of
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
such that
f(Z)
is a point. Let us assume, for the sake of contradiction, that such a
Z
exists. Consider
W:=\pi(Z)
which is a compact positive-dimensional irreducible subvariety of
Y
. Therefore,
{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})]
is a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})
. By the definition of
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
, for any reductive
\varrho:\pi_{1}(Y)\to{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with
[\varrho]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{C})
, we have
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Z^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(X)])=\{1\}
. Therefore,
\varrho({\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(W^{\rm norm})\to\pi_{1}(Y)])
is finite. This contradicts with out assumption that
\mathfrak{C}
is large. Hence,
f
is a one-to-one proper holomorphic map of complex normal spaces. Consequently, it is biholomorphic.
∎

The rest of the proof is same as in
4.32
.
By the proof of
4.31
, there exist

—

a topological Galois unramified covering
q:\widetilde{S}_{H}\to W
, where
W
is a projective normal variety ;

—

a positive closed
(1,1)
-current with continuous potential
T_{0}
over
W
such that
\{T_{0}\}
is Kähler ;

—

a continuous semi-positive plurisubharmonic function
\phi
on
\widetilde{S}_{H}
such that we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}\phi\geq q^{*}T_{0}.

(A.1)

By
A.6
,
\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
can be identified with
\widetilde{S}_{H}
.
Let
p:\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
be the intermediate Galois covering of
Y
between
\widetilde{Y}\to\widetilde{Y}_{\mathfrak{C}}
. By (
A.1
) we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}{\rm d}^{\rm c}p^{*}\phi\geq(q\circ p)^{*}T_{0}.

(A.2)

We apply
proposition

1.14
to conclude that
\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}
is Stein. ∎
